Where to Find the Small Worlds Most Photographers Walk Past

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Where to Find the Small Worlds Most Photographers Walk Past

Peter Baumgarten was looking for centimeter-tall mushrooms when he spotted a cluster of orange caps on a rotting log. A few warm, rainy days had encouraged mushrooms to emerge in Canada’s McLean’s Park, a forest on Ontario’s Manitoulin Island where he regularly walks his dog. At six feet two inches tall, Baumgarten could easily walk past mushrooms that small if he weren’t specifically looking for them.

Full disclosureThis article was brought to you by OM SYSTEM.

Peter Baumgarten is just one of a number of OM SYSTEM ambassadors who specialize in macro photography and shared with PetaPixel their favorite places to look for small subjects and the encounters that keep them returning time and time again. For Lisa and Tom Cuchara, that includes the meadows and woodland around Little River in southern Maine. Chris McGinnis searches the rainforests of Costa Rica and Panama, while also expanding his macro photography portfolio in Maine and his own backyard.

Their photographs show the miniature world that can turn up on a rotting log or a garden stem. Baumgarten gets low on the forest floor to photograph mushrooms and the slugs that feed on them. The Cucharas and McGinnis have both made some of their favorite finds after dark, the Cucharas in a garden in the Northeast and McGinnis at a pond in Panama.

In one of Baumgarten’s favorite macro photographs, a garden slug stretches along rotting wood beneath a cluster of orange mushrooms, its head raised toward one of the caps.

“What excited me the most was the garden slug getting ready to chow down, which isn’t a common shot,” he says.

Baumgarten takes many of his macro shots at a park close to home. McLean’s Park has several kilometers of trails through mixed forest, which he uses for hiking, biking, cross-country skiing, snowshoeing, and photography.

“It is about a 15-minute drive from my home,” he says. “I go there several times a week to walk my dog. As a mature forest there are plenty of fallen trees and branches that make for the perfect environment for a bunch of different types of mushrooms.”

“What I love about the park is the variety of photographs I can take there,” he notes. “One day I might photograph a person walking their dog in autumn along a leaf-covered trail, with tall trees and yellow foliage all around. Another day, I might be down among the fallen logs and branches photographing mushrooms.”

Baumgarten likes to shoot most of his mushroom macro photos below eye level. “I love capturing them from underneath,” he explains. “For me, the gill structure is perhaps the most interesting and photogenic part of the mushroom. I want a soft background that doesn’t pull focus from my subject. By shooting from just below the mushroom I can usually include the more distant, lush greenery of the forest.”

“While mushrooms don’t move, when I’m photographing a slug in the low light of the forest, I have to be aware that even its slow movement can cause blur,” he continues. “Shooting handheld, I might use a shutter speed of around 1/100 second to keep it sharp, raising the ISO if I need to.”

Bumping up the ISO, the camera’s sensitivity to light, lets him shoot at a faster shutter speed when the light on the forest floor is dim. With a mushroom, he can usually keep the ISO low and slow the shutter down instead. The image stabilization in his OM SYSTEM OM-3 lets him handhold exposures as long as half a second, even while using in-camera focus stacking, which combines the sharp areas of photographs focused at different distances to keep more of a close-up subject in focus.

“I capture mushrooms in a very similar way to how I photograph wildlife,” Baumgarten says. “For many wildlife shots I prefer to use my long lens. This gives me great close-up images of my subject. But a great close-up is not always the best way to showcase the animal. Showing the animal in its environment helps me tell more of the story about where it lives. That’s when I turn to using a wider lens. I will often do the same when photographing mushrooms.”

Mushrooms can appear quickly and may not stay in good condition for long, so Baumgarten recommends watching the weather to help decide when to go looking.

“Mushrooms need warm, moist conditions, so I recommend exploring one or two days after a good rain,” he advises. “I have had great success from late spring to late autumn. I recommend avoiding bright sunny days. The dappled sunlight on the forest floor can work for some shots, but often it is more of a hindrance than a help.”

A subject that stays put gives photographers new to macro more time to experiment with focus and composition.

“Photographing mushrooms is a great way to get into macro shooting,” he adds. “I love the geometry of most mushrooms and the infinite patience they have. I’ve never had a mushroom run away from me as I sneak up on it.”

Those quiet searches are also part of why he keeps returning to the forest.

“I often turn to macro photography to get me out of a rut,” Baumgarten says. “I primarily shoot landscapes and wildlife and over the years there have been many stretches of time where I just couldn’t capture shots that I was happy with. It’s at these times, when I feel a bit down, that I grab my macro lens and head into the forest for a bit of exploration and de-stressing. I slow down, observe carefully, and invariably find something in the macro world to photograph. And the best part is, I don’t have to go far. Macro subjects are everywhere, and a local park or garden can be a great place to start looking. I’m lucky to have McLean’s Park just a short drive from home.”

For Lisa and Tom Cuchara, some of the strangest subjects turn up after dark.

“One evening I headed into the garden to look for tiny nighttime wonders,” Lisa Cuchara recalls. “Imagine my surprise when I found a bright green hornworm sporting what looked like dozens of tiny white marshmallows. Those little white blobs weren’t eggs or fungus, though. They were the silken cocoons of tiny parasitoid wasps. The wasp larvae had developed inside the living caterpillar before coming out to spin their cocoons. It was creepy and fascinating at the same time.”

Gardeners don’t like hornworms, since a hungry one can make a massive amount of tomato foliage disappear overnight. The wasps, most likely a Cotesia species, are another matter.

“This was a perfect example of nature’s own pest control,” Lisa explains. “Unlike regular parasites, which generally don’t kill their hosts, parasitoids kill the animal they develop in. These tiny wasps help keep hornworm numbers down, which makes them real allies for gardeners.”

“When I saw this scene, I knew that the challenge was getting the detail of both the caterpillar and those wonderfully wonky white cocoons while working handheld in the dark,” Lisa recalls. “Even at f/11, there just isn’t enough depth of field to get everything sharp in a single exposure.”

An aperture of f/11 is a small lens opening that would normally extend depth of field, the zone of a photograph that looks sharp. In-camera focus stacking, used with the Cucharas’ OM-1 Mark II and the M.Zuiko Digital ED 90mm F3.5 Macro IS PRO lens, got around the problem.

“I was able to shoot a focus-stacked image handheld, without setting up a tripod or disturbing my subject,” Lisa notes.

For insects, the Cucharas use a small flash fitted with a diffuser.

“A bare flash gives you harsh highlights and deep shadows, especially on an insect’s shiny exoskeleton,” the Cucharas explain. “Using a diffuser turns that small flash into a much larger, softer light compared to a tiny subject. The flash also helps freeze movement, whether it’s a slight breeze moving a leaf or an insect shifting position.”

“Without a flashlight and a willingness to explore the garden after sunset, I might have walked right past an extraordinary biological story unfolding just inches away,” Lisa adds.

On other outings, the Cucharas have photographed a moth laying eggs at night and a crab spider grabbing an unsuspecting bumblebee. They’ve also watched a caterpillar that had just shed its outer skin, known as a molt.

“A black swallowtail caterpillar will eat its own molt for energy,” they add. “Those spontaneous moments of seeing something so unique are just one reason we enjoy macro photography so much.”

The Cucharas have turned their own yard into a wildlife habitat, replacing most of the lawn with the goal of mainly native plants grown without pesticides or herbicides. They also lead macro and close-up photography retreats at Little River in North Berwick, Maine.

“We love places where you can move just a few feet and find a whole different world,” they say.

Insects are cold-blooded, so how active they are depends on the temperature and conditions around them.

“Pick a rainy, drizzly, or overcast day,” the Cucharas advise. “Insects like to hang out under the leaves and they’re less likely to move. And get there early, because a lot of insects are cold and slow in the early hours, which gives you more time to photograph them.”

“Slow down and look under every leaf,” they continue. “Don’t rush from flower to flower; pick a small area and explore it from a few angles. You start to see a jumping spider on a stalk, holes in a leaf from insects eating, or a curled leaf with a caterpillar inside.”

“If you’re just starting to practice macro photography, a stationary flower with some personality, pollen, or water droplets is a wonderful starting point,” the Cucharas note. “A flower past its prime, a crooked stem, a curled petal, or an oddly shaped mushroom can be a far more compelling story than a flawless blossom. And learn to notice the background, because it’s just as important as the subject.”

“At one of our retreats, six groups rotated through my session to photograph a swallowtail chrysalis on its host plant,” Lisa recalls. “It gave me a chance to talk about fennel and other host plants, and about how to photograph it without disturbing the spot or making it more obvious to predators. No photograph is worth possible harm to the subject, including insects and the plants they’re on.”

Lisa also says that it’s not necessary to have a large piece of land to start creating your own macro photography sanctuary. “I always recommend planting more native and host plants everywhere, even if it’s just a few planters at your condo. Native plants are what attract the insects that look fascinating in a photograph.”

Even with their own garden, the Cucharas still head out to other spots for macro photography. However, unlike static landscape overlooks, macro photography is always delivering something new. “We often visit the same places again and again, and we always come home with different photographs,” the Cucharas explain. “Flowers open and fade, insect species change from month to month, and the light and weather change. Macro photography might be the best genre for photographers who are always looking to shoot, and learn, something new.”

Chris McGinnis has captured some of his favorite macro photographs after dark, in the rainforests of Costa Rica and Panama.

McGinnis says that one of the most interesting subjects in the Central American rainforest is the red-eyed tree frog. “With these frogs, I am constantly surprised with the variety of moments I am able to capture them in, and maybe my favorite moment was on a trip to Panama with fellow OM SYSTEM ambassador Lee Hoy,” McGinnis remembers. “We had some workshop clients with us, and they faded one by one and went off to sleep, but Lee and I stayed out shooting. We were on this little pond, looking for frogs, and we’d found and photographed several. All of a sudden Lee says, ‘Red-eyed tree frogs in amplexus.’ I said, ‘What? What do you mean?’, not knowing what amplexus meant. Lee pointed to a branch and there were two red-eyed tree frogs with the male on top of the female. I’ve photographed hundreds of these frogs before, but I had never seen them do this. The shot was a unique addition to my portfolio, and I learned a new word in the process.”

According to McGinnis, red-eyed tree frogs spend the day higher up in the trees and come down toward the water as the sun goes down, which is why he looks for them at night. But whether it’s day or night, he often chooses to shoot macro photographs with a non-macro lens.

“That night with the frogs was a time when I could have used my M.Zuiko Digital ED 90mm F3.5 Macro IS PRO,” he recalls. “I wanted to be up really close, but I wanted a wider view so the story was the frogs in their environment, as that can make a more interesting shot.”

Instead, he had the M.Zuiko Digital ED 8-25mm F4.0 PRO on his camera that night. It’s a wide-angle zoom that can focus close enough to fill the frame with a frog while still showing the branch the pair was sitting on.

“I don’t think I could ever get tired of searching for red-eyed tree frogs,” he adds. “It’s an addiction for me. I find one, I photograph it until it jumps away or I’ve exhausted every angle I can get. Then I could go back to my room and go to sleep, or I could try to find another one. And I have a really hard time walking away, because that next one might be something I’ve never seen before, like capturing two red-eyed tree frogs in amplexus.”

“The biggest challenge of the rainforest at night is getting lost,” McGinnis warns. “So I always get a guide, as there is incredible value in going out with someone who knows their way around the rainforest. Not to mention the danger hiding on the ground and in the trees. Central America has lots of venomous snakes. You grab the wrong thing, you step in the wrong place, and you could be in trouble. A local guide knows how to keep you from accidentally running into one of them.”

Rain is a given in a rainforest. “Photographing in the rain is challenging because it can be harder to find subjects or keep your clothing dry, but luckily I never have to worry about my camera gear in the rain, which eliminates one of the challenges,” he notes. With IP53-rated weather sealing, his OM-1 Mark II can keep shooting through snow, dust, and rain. “I’ve been shooting days on end in pouring, torrential rain in Central America, and I’ve never had a problem with my gear.”

When McGinnis isn’t finding small subjects in the Central American rainforest, he also helps lead multi-day macro retreats with Lisa and Tom Cuchara and other OM SYSTEM ambassadors, on fellow ambassador Steve Morello’s property in southern Maine, as well as other locations. One of his favorite subjects on Morello’s property is dragonflies.

“If you find a dragonfly as the sun’s coming up, they’re not warm yet,” McGinnis advises. “They are cold and they might have dew on their wings, making it harder for them to fly. They’re almost like sitting ducks at that time, and much easier to photograph than in the heat of the day, when they’re busy hunting.”

He also searches his own backyard after dark, where wolf spiders and other hunting spiders give themselves away. Their eyes have a reflective layer that bounces a flashlight beam straight back, a glint known as eyeshine.

“In my own backyard, I love to photograph spiders and praying mantises after dark,” he continues. “Spiders hunt at night, and they are easier to find than most people realize. If you hold your flashlight up to the side of your head and scan the grass about 10 to 15 feet ahead of you, the eyeballs of a spider will flicker back at you. In the pitch black, all you’re looking for is those little diamonds in the grass. Having the flashlight right to the side of your eye keeps your line of sight in line with the beam of the flashlight.”

Some of McGinnis’s favorite discoveries don’t happen until he’s back at his computer. “I was trying to get this mantis to cooperate, and I ended up with tons of shots of it in different poses.”

“As I was reviewing them, I noticed a little something else in the shot that was invisible to my naked eye,” he continues. “That mite on its head is very, very tiny, just one or two millimeters. That’s actually happened to me many times, and finding those tiny surprises when I zoom in on the computer is one of the best things about macro photography.”

McGinnis says this is a great way for both beginners and seasoned macro photography veterans to practice and capture unique photos. “Go outside with a flashlight and scan the yard, and you’re bound to find something. No matter where you live, especially in the summertime, go out after dark with your camera and a light. On a nice, warm summer night, it’s just really fun. I’m exploring this small world, and through that exploration, new things reveal themselves. That’s what keeps me going back. Even if it’s my backyard, a local park, or a tropical rainforest, I’m always discovering new things and finding interesting stories in the small world that most other photographers walk by.”

The Gap Between Your Expertise and Your Content Marketing Is One of the Most Expensive Disconnects You Can Make

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

Years of insight often stay trapped in private conversations when they could become the most valuable material for building a public brand.

Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.

Spend 30 minutes with an excellent veteran broker and something interesting usually happens. They’ll explain why a specific neighborhood that looks identical to the one next to it consistently outperforms it at resale. They’ll describe the exact moment in a negotiation when a seller’s resolve breaks and how they learned to recognize it 15 years ago. They’ll tell you which renovation projects reliably return value in their market and which ones buyers quietly discount even when they look expensive. They’ll mention the pattern they’ve watched repeat with first-time buyers for two decades that no amount of pre-purchase education seems to prevent.

Then you look at their Instagram and it says JUST SOLD in large letters over a photograph of a front door.

That gap, between what someone actually knows and what they make public, is one of the more expensive disconnects in professional marketing. And it’s not unique to real estate. Lawyers who’ve spent 20 years navigating a specific type of litigation have pattern recognition their clients would genuinely value if it ever made it out of client conversations. Executives who’ve managed through three recessions understand something about organizational behavior that no business school curriculum captures. Financial advisors who’ve watched clients make the same behavioral mistakes across multiple market cycles carry insight that the average investor would pay serious attention to if it were ever made accessible.

The problem is rarely that these people lack something worth saying. It’s that what they know has become so familiar to them that they’ve stopped recognizing it as knowledge.

There’s something that happens when you’ve seen the same pattern play out hundreds of times. It stops feeling like insight and starts feeling like obvious. The broker who’s watched buyers consistently underestimate carrying costs on older properties no longer experiences that as a revelation. It’s just a thing they know. It stopped surprising them around year six. So it doesn’t occur to them that a first-time buyer encountering this reality for the first time might find it genuinely useful to hear before they’re already in contract.

This is the core problem. Expertise normalizes. The more you know about something, the worse you tend to be at judging what’s interesting about what you know. Familiarity is the enemy of recognizing value.

Which means the job of turning expertise into public-facing content is rarely about inventing new ideas. It’s about extraction. Pulling out what’s already there, what’s been sitting in client conversations and private observations for years, and making it visible to people who haven’t spent a decade accumulating the same experience.

Most professional marketing starts with a version of the same question. What should we post this week? A social media manager gets brought in, templates get built, a content calendar gets assembled and the result is a reliable stream of announcements, generic industry commentary, and the kind of motivational content that gets produced when nobody can think of anything specific to say.

A social media manager cannot manufacture 10 years of pattern recognition. They can execute. They can produce. They can distribute. But the raw material has to come from somewhere, and if it isn’t being extracted from the actual expertise of the professional, it gets replaced with something generic that could have come from anyone.

The better starting question is not what we should post. It’s what we actually know that the market hasn’t heard clearly yet. Those are genuinely different questions, and they produce genuinely different answers.

The questions that tend to unlock useful material are specific, and most professionals haven’t been asked them in the context of their public content.

What do clients ask you repeatedly? Repeated questions are a signal. They mean the market is confused about something and hasn’t found a satisfying answer yet. Every repeated question is a piece of content waiting to exist. The broker who gets asked every spring whether it’s better to buy now or wait through summer has answered that question a hundred times privately. They’ve never written it down publicly once.

What does your industry believe that you think is wrong? Contrarian expertise is some of the most valuable material a professional can produce, and most of them are sitting on it quietly. The financial advisor who thinks conventional wisdom about a specific type of account is actively harmful to the clients who follow it. The broker who’s watched a popular renovation strategy underdeliver in their specific market for fifteen years while the rest of the industry keeps recommending it.

What can you predict because you’ve seen it 100 times? Pattern recognition accumulated through repetition is something generalists and newcomers genuinely cannot replicate. The experienced professional who knows which type of seller is going to change their mind about price after the first two weeks on market, or which buyer behavior signals that an offer isn’t going to survive inspection, is carrying proprietary knowledge. Most of them have never thought of it that way.

What seems obvious to you but consistently surprises clients? This is often the richest material. The things that feel too basic to mention are often the things the audience most needs to hear. The curse of expertise is assuming shared context that doesn’t exist.

What happened recently that reminded you of something you learned 10 years ago? This one turns experience into timely commentary without requiring a formal opinion column. It’s just: Here’s what I watched happen this week and here’s the pattern underneath it that I’ve seen before.

Those answers become articles. They become media commentary when a journalist needs a credible source. They become speaking topics. They become the newsletter that clients actually read because it contains something they couldn’t have gotten from a generic industry publication.

Here’s the distinction that matters. Expertise kept inside client conversations helps the specific people sitting across from you at that moment. It builds a reputation slowly, through word of mouth, one relationship at a time.

Expertise that gets externalized travels without you. An article that captures a genuine insight reaches people who have never met you and may never hire you but who now associate your name with a perspective they found useful. A piece of commentary that captures something real about market behavior becomes a credential that a press mention or a referral can point to. The insight that got shared in a client meeting last Tuesday becomes, when written down, something that works on your behalf indefinitely.

Great thought leadership is usually extraction, not creation. The material is already there. Most experts just haven’t been asked the right questions to get it out.

Spend 30 minutes with an excellent veteran broker and something interesting usually happens. They’ll explain why a specific neighborhood that looks identical to the one next to it consistently outperforms it at resale. They’ll describe the exact moment in a negotiation when a seller’s resolve breaks and how they learned to recognize it 15 years ago. They’ll tell you which renovation projects reliably return value in their market and which ones buyers quietly discount even when they look expensive. They’ll mention the pattern they’ve watched repeat with first-time buyers for two decades that no amount of pre-purchase education seems to prevent.

Then you look at their Instagram and it says JUST SOLD in large letters over a photograph of a front door.

That gap, between what someone actually knows and what they make public, is one of the more expensive disconnects in professional marketing. And it’s not unique to real estate. Lawyers who’ve spent 20 years navigating a specific type of litigation have pattern recognition their clients would genuinely value if it ever made it out of client conversations. Executives who’ve managed through three recessions understand something about organizational behavior that no business school curriculum captures. Financial advisors who’ve watched clients make the same behavioral mistakes across multiple market cycles carry insight that the average investor would pay serious attention to if it were ever made accessible.

Why Germany’s Top Stock Exchange Predicts $9 XRP

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Home » Ripple (XRP) » Why Germany’s Top Stock Exchange Predicts $9 XRP

Regulatory licensing momentum & XRPL’s intended role as core settlement infrastructure serve as key catalysts.

Some weeks ago, Germany’s Sparkassen savings bank, serving ~50 million customers with €1.62T in assets under management (AUM), began a phased roll-out of XRP trading in their banking apps. Now, a crew member of Oliver Michel of Tokentus Investment AG shared his personal bullish view on XRP price, setting a long-term target between $7 to $9.

On DER AKTIONÄR TV, the Frankfurt Stock Exchange’s affiliate dropped some knowledge in regards to Ripple’s long-term vision of revolutionizing & optimizing the classic financial system, as seen in recent examples. Just last month, Ripple inked a traditional trust bank license in the United States (USA), edging closer towards a full-scale XRPL implementation.

BREAKING 🚨: The largest stock exchange in Germany says that #XRP will be the backbone of the new financial system! He also says that XRP could hit $7-$9 pretty soon, and might even soar to over $100 once the system runs on XRPL! pic.twitter.com/ptED0SNEh5

Oliver Michel expresses confidence in XRP ecosystem long-term prospects, suggesting that their most significant developments could take some time to materialize. The daily XRP charts hint at a consolidation phase now, the analyst notes, but then he named a three-headed snake that could turn out as the pivotal catalyst in the bounce back rally.

As strongest points, he references an electronic money license and notes over 60 obtained licenses worldwide. Surely, a $9 price target for XRP would mean nearly tripling the current all-time high (ATH). Set at $3.65, the OG altcoin took a nosedive towards $1.20 earlier this week, but managed to stay afloat at the $1.24 major demand zone since November.

The intersection between traditional finance & digital assets could boost the global crypto market cap by billions, while DLT-based chains have an edge in terms of adoption due to the ISO 20022 compliance.

November 2026 additions include a new co-op adventure, a kingdom-building game, and a familiar fantasy universe

Among the biggest additions are Escape Academy 2: Back 2 School, Thronefall, and Warhammer Survivors, with all three arriving before the middle of November. Here's what to know about these games and everything else coming to Xbox Game Pass.

One of the biggest additions is Escape Academy 2: Back 2 School, the sequel to Coin Crew Games' 2022 puzzle adventure. Arriving Nov. 5, the game expands the escape room formula into an open-world experience, with online and local two-player co-op allowing friends to explore a school campus and solve puzzles together.

Another notable addition is Warhammer Survivors, an officially licensed bullet-heaven game developed by Auroch Digital in partnership with Vampire Survivors creator poncle. Launching Nov. 10, it brings Warhammer champions, enemy factions, and weapons into the survival formula, with players unleashing barrages of bullets, blades, and magic.

Ubisoft Makes Bid to Purchase Xbox Studio on the Brink of Closure

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Xbox is 'exploring options' for Arkane Studios

Microsoft intends to divest from Dishonored and Marvel's Blade developer Arkane Studios as part of its mass layoffs at Xbox and other Microsoft divisions. Because Arkane is based in Lyon, France, Xbox has had to adhere to French labor laws, and was mandated to search for a potential buyer for Arkane.

Assassin's Creed maker Ubisoft has reportedly made an offer for Arkane, Bloomberg reported on Tuesday. Ubisoft is one of at least three companies to submit a bid for Arkane. According to Bloomberg, Xbox is also considering selling Arkane to the studio's management.

The future of Arkane looked in doubt last month as negotiations for a redundancy plan for potentially laid-off employees were reportedly not going well ahead of a Nov. 13 deadline. That deadline for Arkane to find a new home was extended, and Xbox is "exploring options" for what to do with Arkane ahead of its December deadline, Bloomberg said. Xbox has not accepted any offer at this time.

Marvel's Blade was announced almost three years ago at the 2023 The Game Awards. Arkane has been quiet about it since, and its internal ship date was delayed until late 2027.

If Arkane avoids being shut down by Microsoft and gets sold elsewhere, it will likely be able to wrap up development on its game starring The Daywalker. According to Bloomberg, Blade is "expected to be released within the next year or two."

Blade is a fan-favorite Marvel character who's a dhampir, a hybrid of a vampire and a human. He's appeared in Marvel's Midnight Suns, Marvel Tokon: Fighting Souls, Marvel Rivals, and more.

Scientists discover a hidden pattern in the sun’s chaotic magnetic field

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The solar wind's magnetic field twists one way at one scale and the other way at a smaller one, and the pattern drifts outward.

Stir milk into coffee and the swirls that follow have no plan. The stream of charged particles flowing from the sun is turbulent, too, with magnetic fields twisting through the chaos.

Scientists have long struggled to find order in these turbulent magnetic fields.

The twist has a pattern. In measurements from NASA’s Parker Solar Probe, the field turns one way at one size of ripple and the opposite way at ripples twice as small.

The pair of opposite twists sits right where the gas stops behaving like a fluid and starts behaving like a crowd of separate particles.

Masatomi Iizawa of TU Braunschweig led the analysis with Nobumitsu Yokoi of the University of Tokyo and colleagues in ten countries. Among them is Stuart Bale of UC Berkeley, who leads the instrument that took the readings.

“We see a clear structure in the spectrum, even though the turbulence represents unstructured motions,” said Iizawa, the paper’s first author.

A magnetic field in moving gas points somewhere, and it also turns.

As a ripple passes a detector, the field direction rotates, either clockwise or counterclockwise with respect to the gas’s direction of travel.

Physicists call that sense of rotation magnetic helicity. In the big, slow ripples of the solar wind, it has looked like nothing but noise since the 1980s.

Clockwise and counterclockwise turned up in roughly equal measure, which is what randomness looks like.

Sharp peaks near the smallest ripples have shown up before, in data from earlier spacecraft.

Following one as the gas traveled was the part no one could do, because older missions sampled too slowly to catch ripples that fast.

Parker Solar Probe samples fast enough. The team used its magnetometer readings from late September to late November 2021, an hour at a time.

Over those weeks the spacecraft swung between 0.1 and 0.78 times the Earth-sun distance.

Close to the sun, at a tenth of that distance, the spectrum held two spikes rather than noise.

One spike sat at 10 ripples a second and carried the twist one way. The other sat at 20 ripples a second and carried it the other way.

The two peaks revealed an organized pattern that random fluctuations alone could not readily explain.

Two opposite twists sitting side by side is what plasma physicists call a bihelical structure, and until now it was something theory predicted and data rarely showed.

Comparing measurements at different distances from the sun, the researchers found that both peaks shifted toward slower frequencies, corresponding to larger structures.

The drift held from 0.1 out to about 0.4 of the Earth-sun distance.

Gas expands as it travels, so some drift was expected. Simple expansion predicts a particular shape to it, with the frequency falling in step with distance.

The measured drift fell off exponentially instead, far faster than expansion accounts for.

The researchers then checked the other obvious explanation. If the twists were made and maintained by local conditions, their position should track the local scales set by protons in the gas.

Scaled against those, the peaks slid right past them.

The team calls that behavior nonlocal. The findings suggest that interactions across different scales, rather than local plasma conditions alone, may be driving the changes.

Turbulence normally hands energy down a ladder, from big swirls to small ones, until the smallest turn into heat. The same ladder turns the tiny vortices seen on the sun’s own surface.

One recent theory holds that at the boundary between fluid behavior and particle behavior, the twist itself jams that ladder and forces energy back up to larger scales.

A pile-up of magnetic twist at scales bigger than the local proton scales is exactly what the jam would produce.

The team’s observation fits that prediction, and it arrives as a measurement rather than a simulation.

Yokoi said the result matters for plasma physics and astrophysics alike.

Understanding this turbulence could eventually help scientists improve models of the solar wind and its effects on Earth’s magnetic environment.

Past roughly half the Earth-sun distance, the two spikes faded out.

The probe’s orbit stays within a few degrees of the plane the planets share, and around there it keeps crossing the current sheet dividing the sun’s magnetic field in two.

Crossing that sheet may scramble the structure, or the fade may just be where the spacecraft happened to be.

One spacecraft measures one point, so the twist it records is a slice of the full three-dimensional quantity.

Turning ripple timings into ripple sizes rests on an assumption about how fast the gas carries them past. The team puts the possible error there at a factor of about 2.5.

Nobody could watch the energy move, either. That would take readings of the gas’s speed as fast as the magnetic ones, and particle instruments can’t scan that quickly in gas this thin.

No one has caught these structures forming, or followed one outward in a single parcel of gas.

Iizawa’s team suggests Europe’s Solar Orbiter, which flies above and below the planets’ plane, could settle whether the fade is real or an accident of one orbit.

The full study was published in the journal The Astrophysical Journal Letters.

Man United 3-2 Roma PLAYER RATINGS: Which star delivered their best performance in a red shirt? Whose physicality is something to marvel at? And who deserves 3/10 for their abysmal defending?

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Published: 17:21 EDT, 3 November 2026 | Updated: 20:11 EDT, 3 November 2026

Manchester United pulled out an enormous win for Michael Carrick as they fought back from 2-0 down to beat Roma 3-2 in the Champions League.

Winless in nine games heading into this European night under the lights and United were staring at 10 without a win, a record that was last struck in 1978, when they were two down at half-time after a dreadful start.

But United conjured up spirit that has been barely visible so far this season as a brace from Matheus Cunha - who finished the night getting sent off - followed a penalty from Bruno Fernandes to seal the most crucial of wins.

What it means for Carrick's future in the medium to longer term remains to be seen.

Those upstairs at United have privately taken a lot of the blame and want to end the churn of managers carrying the can for poor player performances. Only time will tell how big a turning point this night can be.

Daily Mail Sport's NATHAN SALT was at Old Trafford on a frantic night to run the rule over the players...

Matheus Cunha rescued victory from the jaws of defeat for Manchester United against Roma

Made a big save low to his right on the stroke of half-time to prevent further embarrassment for United and, as it turned out, give them a fighting chance of getting the result they pulled out of the hat. His distribution seems to have really regressed this season with far too many aimless balls, though.

Made a huge recovery tackle to deny Rodrigo Mora a third and in a feckless first half it was he who bust a gut where others didn't. Yes, it's a low bar but Yoro has actually been one of, if not, the best United performers during this never-ending malaise.

A performance littered with mistakes that, in truth, looked to have been scrubbed from his game coming into this campaign. He's the leader of the back-line and he did everything but lead in a first half that saw positioning and decision-making leave a lot to be desired. His clearance that resulted in Roma's second goal was diabolical. Booked and was fortunate not to have been sent off.

Harry Maguire left a lot to be desired in a defensive performance littered with basic errors

Thrust into the side in place of Lisandro Martinez and his head will have been spinning when he gave away a penalty for a handball, which Donyell Malen converted. Booked for a lunging challenge that is becoming a worrying trend of tackling with studs up. May sound daft but he needs coaching by Jonny Evans or Jonathan Woodgate on a better way to slide tackle. Booked.

Abysmal defending for Roma's first goal as he committed to the attack like a winger and left Matias Soule the freedom of Manchester to score. Kept pushing forward – you have to assume by instruction – and kept being found wanting. Options are slim but he's not making any positive contribution to the team from left back. Needs a stop putting to it.

Got told to 'f**k off' by Bruno Fernandes in a fraught first half for a bobbly pass to tee him up on the edge of the box. Made a couple of smart bursts through lines but he's in the side to protect the defence and he didn't do it. Night cut short when he pulled his hamstring 60 seconds into the second half.

Will be disappointed with himself at how sloppily he gave away the ball in a turnover which exposed his defenders for the first goal. That error aside, he settled in and improved as it wore on. His ability to actually beat a man is in short supply in this United side and his physicality was something to marvel at at times.

Kobbie Mainoo actually looks to drive past players, a skill in short supply at United

Always has a buzz about him when he gets the ball and he was the only one to come back from Como with any crumb of credit. Here it was a more sobering affair where his influence was heavily restricted and it means his wait for a United goal goes on. Not managed one since December 2025.

A night of frustration for large spells but one that did eventually serve up a goal and assist for the United captain. He was unfairly booked when Soule got into a pushing match that was very one sided down in the corner. Missed a second-half penalty (which was re-taken due to encroachment) and made no mistake second time around. All that aside, it was a poor look when he's berating team-mates when his own performance level is way below what he's capable of, mind you.

Comfortably the best player in a United shirt on the night - even with the red card. For one, he gets stuck in. For two, it was Cunha who won the penalty – however fortunate a decision that was – that Fernandes eventually converted to make it 2-1. He deserved an assist when he set Mbeumo free for a run on goal moments later. Scored two crucial late goals that will airbrush just how bad a tackle he made to see red. Could make a case for his best performance in a United shirt. It's up there.

Had his head in his hands, much like a lot of the supporters, when he struck both posts after being played through one v one by Cunha. Had a couple of opportunities to scamper through on goal but just didn't have the pace.

Bryan Mbeumo will want his second half breakaway chance back after striking both posts

Muted in celebration at the end and given everything he's had to navigate this season that's perhaps understandable. But this was a shot in the arm, how big we need to wait and see. People have labelled him a dead man walking but he's not giving up yet.

Youri Tielemans (for Carlos Baleba, 51) - 5

Did OK, but didn't do a great deal to drive the play forward. Not exactly the perfect foil to Mainoo, who is stylistically similar.

Lifted the crowd and helped change the game. Should have won a penalty when he was hacked down and it was his assist to Cunha that sealed victory. Coudln't have made a stronger case to start on Sunday.

Lisandro Martinez (for Ayden Heaven, 75) - 6

The fact he was far away from the eventual headlines was probably a good thing for all concerned after what transpired at Stamford Bridge.

Dior and <i><em>Harper’s Bazaar</em></i> Host a Screening of the New Richard Avedon Documentary in Los Angeles

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

With Emma Watson, Gia Coppola, and more in attendance

From a Paris Fashion Week toast to a New York City launch party, Dior and Harper’s Bazaar have been honoring the legacy of fashion photographer Richard Avedon these past few weeks. This series of intimate events has been tied to the launch of a new coffee-table book titled Richard Avedon: The Harper’s Bazaar Years, as well as a documentary film, Avedon. Now, the festivities have traveled to Los Angeles.

Avedon, who passed away in 2004 at the age of 81, was one of the most influential fashion photographers of the 20th century. In 1944, at just 21 years old, he started his career at Harper’s Bazaar under the guidance of then-editor-in-chief Carmel Snow, and he spent the next two-plus decades defining the creative vision of the publication.

Richard Avedon: The Harper’s Bazaar Years (available now) explores this artistic period by compiling rarely seen photographs, a never-before-published account from Avedon himself, a foreword by editor-in-chief Samira Nasr, and an essay by Bazaar contributing editor Derek C. Blasberg. The new documentary, meanwhile, offers a look through the Avedon archives from a different lens. It was directed by Academy Award–winning filmmaker Ron Howard (Apollo 13, A Beautiful Mind, The Da Vinci Code).

Focusing on the film, Dior and Blasberg hosted a private screening of Avedon at the William Morris Endeavor headquarters in L.A. Guests such as Emma Watson, Gia Coppola, Lauren Hutton, Devon Lee Carlson, and more gathered to watch the film before heading to the House of Dior in Beverly Hills for a cocktail reception.

Data centers are turning to old jet engines as the wait for power grows

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Stuck in years-long lines for grid connections and new turbines, some AI data centers are making their own electricity with converted jet engines, the New York Times reported Tuesday.

The engines, pulled from retired aircraft and rebuilt to burn natural gas, aren’t anyone’s idea of a permanent power plant. But they’re another sign of how far power supply has fallen behind the AI buildout.

For companies stuck in those lines, the main draw of converted jet engines is how quickly the engines can be up and running. “It’s all about getting power as fast as possible,” Rystad Energy analyst Audun Martinsen told the Times. With jet-powered units, “they can have a data center running in 24 months,” he said.

Hooking up 100 megawatts to the grid in Northern Virginia, by comparison, takes about seven years, according to the International Energy Agency. Most power capacity is expected to come on by 2031, an executive at Mitsubishi Power, one of the world’s top makers of power generation equipment, told Bloomberg last week.

In some cases, these delays are already costing some data center operators business. Last month, Microsoft pulled out as the anchor customer for Nscale’s data center in Loughton, England, after the local utility said it couldn’t deliver enough power until the early to mid-2030s.

Elon Musk’s xAI is among the companies already using the engines, having installed eight converted jet-engine turbines in Mississippi to help power its Colossus 2 data center in Memphis, the Times reported, citing state permits and satellite imagery. The units come from ProEnergy, which rebuilds a GE engine commonly found on Boeing jets.

In 2025, OpenAI followed Musk’s lead with gas turbines at its first Stargate site in Abilene, Texas. Crusoe, a Denver-based startup that builds AI data centers, is constructing that Abilene campus and ordered 13 of ProEnergy’s converted units in April.

In September, Crusoe walked away from a $1.25 billion deal to buy 29 natural gas turbines from Boom Supersonic, a startup developing a supersonic passenger jet that has also begun selling a version of that jet’s engine as a power plant for data centers. A Crusoe spokesperson told TechCrunch at the time that the company still plans to use turbines.

Currently the Trump administration is adding to the pressure by encouraging data centers to generate their own electricity.

Converted jet engines come with trade-offs, though: They’re less efficient and pollute more than turbines designed for conventional gas plants, and it takes more of them to make the same amount of power, according to the Times. That may ultimately make converted jet engines a short-term play with some experts saying that efficiency and environmental concerns may grow in importance in a year or two.

The fallout from last month's departure of Xbox's newly appointed COO just got a little messier.

ICYMI, late last month, Helen Chiang, who had been with Microsoft for 21 years and was appointed COO in July as part of the big Xbox reset, abruptly left the company. Chiang has yet to provide an explanation for her departure, and the conspicuously brief memo in which Xbox CEO Asha Sharma announced Chiang's departure only deepened the mystery. This week, Chiang herself shot down reports that she was fired for violating policy and said she had "serious concerns" about Microsoft's handling of the situation, but notably didn't directly refute claims she was ousted.

Now, a prominent figure in Xbox's history is stepping up in defense of Chiang with a sharp rebuke of Microsoft leadership. In a response to Chiang on LinkedIn (captured via Twitter user Knoebel and independently verified), former Xbox VP Shannon Loftis, who was with Microsoft for 33 years before leaving in 2022, throws her full support behind Chiang amid her departure.

"Team Helen all the way," says Loftis. "The 'leak' itself (of false information, for what purpose -? Let's all speculate on who made THAT decision) shows the loss of moral character and 'clarity' in leadership at Xbox. A real leadership team would have relentlessly pursued the 'leaker' and taken appropriate action because 'confidential personnel matters' actually are bases for defamation lawsuits."

The "leaker" Loftis is referring to appears to be the source at Eurogamer who had claimed Chiang was fired for "policy violations." Chiang has vehemently denied the claim about why she was fired, but so far Xbox and Microsoft have provided no official statement beyond the published internal memo from Sharma, which provides virtually zero explanation for Chiang's departure.

"It's a clownshow up in there and I feel terrible for anyone that still has to deal with it," Loftis concludes.

Loftis is a self-employed "video game maker" today, according to her LinkedIn, but she originally joined Microsoft as an IT project manager way back in 1993, before hopping over to the games side of the business in 1995. She would climb all the way up to VP of Xbox Game Studios in 2022, a position she would hold for just a few months.

Unai Emery has closed gap between Aston Villa and Europe's elite - but rampant Barcelona proved there is still a way to go as Lamine Yamal dazzles, writes OLIVER HOLT

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Published: 17:17 EDT, 3 November 2026 | Updated: 02:35 EDT, 4 November 2026

John McGinn heard the question. His eyes darted around Barcelona's packed media auditorium for a second or two on Monday night. What was it like to be about to play in the Nou Camp, the Aston Villa skipper had been asked, as if this were to be some sort of spin off from Bill and Ted's Excellent Adventure.

McGinn was careful with his answer. He did not seek to downplay the nature of the occasion or the thrill that comes with lining up in such a majestic arena. But he was determined not to play the wide-eyed ingenu. 'What our club is trying to do now,' McGinn said, 'is make this normal, make this a yearly experience.'

So when he and his team-mates walked up the flight of steps from the dressing rooms into the vast bowl of the stadium on Tuesday evening, they were here to show that they were not arrivistes. Villa is a historic club. They won this competition in 1982, ten years before Barcelona ever did. They were not here for a treat or to get patted on the back just for turning up.

It is nearly 20 years since Liverpool became the last English club to win here and nobody gave Villa too much chance of ending that drought. They are being told constantly that the odds are stacked against them and that their much-admired manager, Unai Emery, may be tempted by the entreaties of struggling 'giants' elsewhere.

That was the reason why this game early in the league phase had attained so much importance. For Villa, it was a symbol of something they aspire to. They want to get used to belonging in this company, to walking up those steps, to emerging into the stadium in the pouring rain, and staring at players of the calibre of Lamine Yamal, Rodri and Pau Cubarsi staring back at them.

It still feels as if they stand on the cusp of something bigger but they blew their shot at proving that on Tuesday night. They needed to be at their best to have a chance of beating Hansi Flick's side, which sits atop LaLiga, and they were far too naïve in defence to get close to causing a shock. They are rising but this was a step too far at this stage of their evolution.

Lamine Yamal (centre) and Barcelona had plenty to smile about after beating Aston Villa 3-0

Yamal opened the scoring for Barcelona after just seven minutes with this left-footed finish

Raphinha (right) doubled Barca's lead after half-time with this strike at the Spotify Nou Camp

The Nou Camp, which is still in the midst of an extensive renovation and was only about two-thirds full, was ringed by towering yellow cranes, peering down at the floodlit patch of green. From their lofty vantage point, they saw Villa get off to the worst possible start.

Only seven minutes had gone when Emery's team gift-wrapped a present for the home side. Zion Suzuki played the ball out short to Joao Gomes but Gomes panicked under pressure from the Barcelona press and the close attention of Pedri. He played the ball square without looking, straight to Yamal.

Of all the players he could have found, Gomes had to find Yamal. The Spain superstar squared up Pau Torres and then curled the ball around him with a gentle caress of his left boot. Suzuki was helpless. The ball nestled in the corner of the net. Barcelona had scored 56 goals in 14 games before this match. Extending the hand of generosity to them must have felt particularly galling for Emery.

Villa almost hit back immediately. A stinging volley from Matty Cash was deflected wide and Barcelona looked jittery at the back. Nicolas Jackson played a square ball to Johan Manzambi, who should have equalised but scuffed his first-time effort wide of Joan Garcia. Barcelona were thankful for Garcia's intervention when he raced out of his goal to dispossess Jackson as he charged on to a through ball.

Yamal was rampant. His battle with Ian Maatsen was a mismatch and Maatsen was booked after 11 minutes for hacking him down. Villa were still intent on playing themselves into trouble. Suzuki repeated his mistake when he tried to play the ball out to Joao Gomes. This time, Pedri beat him to it and the loose ball fell to Gordon. Gordon hit his shot towards the corner but Suzuki redeemed himself by diving to push it away.

Barcelona had the better of the play. They looked like scoring every time they went forward. Time and again, either Raphinha or Yamal found space to run at the Villa defence, which was barely coping. Even in that context, though, it was an open game. Villa had chances, too. Manzambi forced a fine save out of Garcia. A Jackson breakaway was thwarted by a last-ditch block.

But seven minutes after the break, Barcelona doubled their lead. Fermin Lopez played a clever ball to Raphinha, who had found a yard of space in the box. Raphinha, LaLiga's leading scorer this season, drilled a left-foot shot goalwards and it deflected off the heel of Torres, wrong-footed Suzuki and rolled over the line.

Barcelona put the game out of reach midway through the half when Joao Cancelo forced his way past McGinn on the left flank and hit a sumptuous cross into the box with the outside of his right foot. Raphinha gambled on the path of the ball, darted behind his marker and tucked the ball away.

Raphinha completed the scoreline with this cute finish as Barcelona completed their victory

For Aston Villa, this will be a learning curve as they look to go far in the Champions League

Villa manager Unai Emery will have plenty to think about after another Nou Camp defeat

Substitute Leon Goretzka hit the face of a post for Villa late on but the game was already gone. There was no shame in losing to a team who are one of the favourites to win the competition this season but there was frustration in the way Villa had handed their opponents the advantage.

Emery looked bedraggled as he stood on the touchline in the rain, arms folded, shaking his head. This was his 15th defeat in 16 visits to the Nou Camp. As Villa's coach fights to edge his team closer to the elite of the game, this game will become just one more bad memory.

Skip The McDonald's Egg McMuffin: The Fast Food Breakfast Sandwich With Thick, Crispy Bacon

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

When it comes to fast food breakfast sandwiches, McDonald's remains uppermost in many people's minds. And why not? The iconic Egg McMuffin, with its famously round egg patty, has been the cornerstone of the chain's breakfast menu since the 1970s. And while you can sub the Canadian bacon that comes standard on the McMuffin for regular bacon, Wendy's Breakfast Baconator actually takes the pork product to a whole new level if that's your sandwich protein preference. And more than that, it ranks as one of the best overall fast food breakfast sandwiches, based on the opinions of the customers who eat them.

Wendy's, which has only served breakfast nationwide on a permanent basis since 2020, is doing the morning meat right with its Breakfast Baconator, which features not only six strips of thick, crispy applewood-smoked bacon, but also a sausage patty — which, like the burgers, comes in a square shape — a freshly cracked egg cooked into a patty (Wendy's is one of the rare fast food chains that use real, whole eggs), two slices of American cheese, and a Swiss cheese sauce. Then, everything gets piled onto not a breakfast bread, like a bagel or English muffin, but a potato roll, which is perhaps better suited to hold the hefty fillings. This symphony of flavors and textures led one user on Reddit to call the immensely bacon-forward sandwich "peak fast food breakfast" that "simply just [can't] get any better," a sentiment echoed by plenty of fellow fans.

The Breakfast Baconator is pretty beloved as-is, but there are ways to modify the menu item and make it that much more enjoyable. One Redditor had the idea of actually stuffing more into the sandwich by adding Wendy's seasoned potato wedges and a flavorful sauce, writing that "it'll change your life." We think the Scorchin' Hot sauce would be an especially tasty addition.

Further, if the potato bun is too lunch-y for you, you can swap it out for a breakfast bread — try asking for the Breakfast Baconator on a biscuit. It's a modification that cannot be made on the app or online, so you'll have to verbally request the change when you place your order at the drive-thru or the counter, if your location still offers that option. Of course, if there simply isn't enough of something on the Breakfast Baconator for your tastes, you can add up to four more sausage patties, four more eggs, and extra Swiss cheese sauce.

Oil Companies Will Love That The 2027 Chevrolet Silverado's New V8s Get Worse Gas Mileage Than The Old Ones

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Due to a litany of misguided decisions on behalf of the Trump administration, gas is ridiculously expensive right now, corporate fuel economy requirements are worryingly low, and we're in an affordability crisis. In totally unrelated news, the oil and gas companies that spent millions to elect Trump are raking in record profits that are more than double what they were at this time last year. Those oil companies are doing fine, but they will still likely be stoked to learn that GM announced the fuel economy numbers for V8-powered examples of the freshly redesigned 2027 Chevrolet Silverado today, and they all get worse gas mileage than the outgoing Silverado's V8s. These numbers should all also apply to the 2027 GMC Sierra, too.

According to documents posted by GM Authority, the fuel economy of all Silverados equipped with either the new 5.7-liter V8 or the new 6.6-liter V8 has dropped between 1 and 2 mpg across city ratings, highway ratings, and combined ratings. While these drops aren't especially significant, it's rare to see redesigned versions of cars debut with worse efficiency than their predecessors, even when we're talking about big trucks.

2027 Silverados fitted with the 5.7-liter V8 are rated at 16 mpg city, 19 mpg highway, and 17 combined in two-wheel-drive form; opting for four-wheel drive drops both the highway and combined ratings by an additional mpg. Those city and combined figures are each down one mpg, and the highway rating is down two mpg compared to the 2026 Silverado.

Trucks with the 6.6-liter V8 are only shown in four-wheel-drive, and are rated at 14 mpg in the city, 18 mpg on the highway, and 15 mpg combined. If you go for the off-road-oriented Silverado ZR2, it drops the city and combined figures by 1 mpg and the highway figure by 3 mpg. (The same should go for the Sierra's AT4X equivalent.) All of those numbers are down by either 1 or 2 mpg when compared to their fuel economy ratings in 2026.

Even opting for the 3.0-liter inline-six turbodiesel powertrain option won't save as much fuel as it did last year. Most diesel-powered 2027 Silverados return between 1 and 2 mpg worse than last year, except for city mileage in some four-wheel-drive trims. City and highway mileage for the diesel ZR2 remains the same. Mileage ratings for the turbocharged 2.7-liter four-cylinder powertrain have not been published yet.

Considering that the Chevrolet Silverado almost always lands in the second-place spot on the list of best-selling vehicles in the U.S. behind the Ford F-Series, and the GMC Sierra isn't too far behind, the impact of small changes in efficiency get magnified. So far through the third quarter of 2026, GMC sold 252,639 Sierras and Chevrolet sold 413,383 Silverados, and production of the new-gen trucks only just started in October. Though not all of these trucks will be fitted with one of the new V8s, certainly a ton of them will, and decreasing even just V8 gas mileage by a single mpg will still result in a massive increase in collective fuel usage.

Man United conjured a miraculous comeback against Roma that was thrilling, visceral and raw... finally Michael Carrick has something to cling to, writes IAN LADYMAN

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Published: 17:15 EDT, 3 November 2026 | Updated: 03:19 EDT, 4 November 2026

Once again they sang Michael Carrick's name and this time it was not out of sympathy. This time it was different.

This time there had been some guts and this time there had been some glory. This time, most importantly, there had been a victory. So the Stretford End sang Carrick's name out of thanks. Finally some respite from the relentless misery of this tortuous season.

How Carrick's Manchester United team pulled off this comeback is hard to fathom. But for now all that matters to Carrick is that they did. For at last he has something tangible to cling to as he tries to drag his club's season back from the jaws of hell.

How on earth do we describe this game? Maybe just with the facts.

Carrick's team were so bad in the first half it was almost impossible to understand. However awful they have been this season, this was worse. Two down to early goals from Matias Soule and Donyell Malen, United presented both to Roma on a plate.

The game looked over. United's Champions League season looked over. Carrick's reign felt over.

Matheus Cunha's late double rescued Manchester United from the jaws of defeat on a night of madness at Old Trafford

Cunha scored in the 83rd and 86th minute to turn the game on its head against Roma

There was still time for Cunha to be sent off in the dying embers of the game - but that failed to take the shine off a memorable night

In the crowd they waved green and gold balloons in protest at the owners the Glazers. Then they threw tennis balls on to the field. The only surprise was that none of the United players put one in their own net.

But then, somehow and almost impossibly, this game changed. Whether it will be enough to prompt a genuine shift in the course of United's season, only time will tell. The chances are it won't. There is too much wrong.

They were lucky to be given an early second-half penalty and lucky that Bruno Fernandes was given the chance to take it again after he missed the first one. They were then lucky in the 83rd minute when Matheus Cunha was handed an equaliser by a defensive howler.

But luck is no good if you don't ride it. And the winning goal Cunha delivered into the net at the near post with four minutes left was not about luck. It was about a team and a player seizing the opportunity to write a different story.

It was born of desire, desperation and perhaps some anger after the pain of the last few weeks. Within a second, the Brazilian's shirt was off and he was in the crowd.

It was hard to blame Cunha, even if he was to pay a price. Booked for that, he was soon cautioned again for a foul and was forced to leave the scene early. At least it was to an ovation. We have not heard one of those here for a while.

Old Trafford was jumping. Of course it was. Thus far this season has brought United supporters only embarrassment and at times earlier here this had felt like a new low. The way United played in the first half was arguably worse than anything we saw under Ruben Amorim and Erik ten Hag.

Matias Soule had given Roma the lead after only 11 minutes

The game was briefly halted in the first-half when fans threw objects including tennis balls onto the pitch

United boss Michael Carrick suffered a further blow when Carlos Baleba limped off

United were in full self-harm mode and in truth could have been four down by half-time.

There was actually nothing in this game for the first 10 minutes but United hurtled from safety to dire straits in the space of seven minutes. That's the way it tends to go and the first Roma goal was simply extraordinary.

When United midfielder Kobbie Mainoo lost the ball in his own half, his team found themselves a defender short. Diogo Dalot, the left back, was so far up field he may as well have been on the Stretford End.

When Rodrigo Mora switched the ball across into the space where Dalot should have been, Soule had time to cut inside and shoot low in to Senne Lammens' bottom left hand corner.

It was one of those goals that looked stranger the more you studied it. Six minutes later, United presented Roma with another one.

Once again Dalot was out of position as the ball reached goalscorer Soule on the edge of the penalty area. This time only a sliding block from Leny Yoro denied the Argentine his second goal but the ball had struck the defender's raised arm and former Aston Villa forward Malen slid in the penalty.

United were shambolic. Fernandes was booked for being, well, Fernandes. It was a harsh card but sadly the United captain's reputation now precedes him. It's the way it works in sport.

As half-time neared, Malen brought a low save from Lammens before Soule curled one from 18 yards that must have missed the righthand post by no more than six inches.

That was the difference between United being out of the game and still in with the chance of the kind of comeback they eventually produced.

Cunha was desperately fortunate to be awarded the penalty that started it all just before the hour. He seemed to trip himself up. Fernandes was then lucky to be offered a retake after his first kick was saved. The level of encroachment by a Roma defender didn't seem to meet the threshold written in law.

But once Fernandes smashed the retake high into the net Roma suddenly found themselves facing a different home team and a different environment.

Bruno Fernandes' first penalty was saved - but he made amends with his second after the referee ordered a retake for encroachment

Victory eases the pressure on United boss Michael Carrick

Carrick will perhaps argue he deserves some fortune. He certainly didn't get any as his team lost at Chelsea at the weekend. Nevertheless, his team needed some more.

Bryan Mbeumo had struck both posts with one shot after running clear immediately after Fernandes' goal while a header from Harry Maguire at a corner hit an opponent when it appeared to be heading in.

But United's fire seemed to be on the verge of burning out when a harmless 83rd-minute cross from Fernandes was completely missed by Roma defender Leonardo Balerdi to present Cunha with an equaliser from six yards.

Roma were by now a shell of the team that they had been in the first half. They had been knocked from their feet by a red tidal wave, and when Cunha unleashed a hammer shot from an angle three minutes later they were dead.

Valve is making changes to deal with a ‘significant increase’ in Steam game submissions

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Valve admits that it’s now taking ‘up to 12 business days’ to review games.

Valve admits that it’s now taking ‘up to 12 business days’ to review games.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

In a new post today, Valve acknowledged that it’s seen “a significant increase in submissions for store and page review in recent months” and says it’s “running a little behind” by taking “up to 12 business days” for reviews instead of the typical 3-5 days.

Indie developers have recently expressed frustrations over lengthy approval times for Steam games, even causing some games to miss planned launch dates. Valve says that “much” of the delay is having to re-review games, so it’s making some changes to the release checklist for developers to hopefully “cut down on re-reviews for basic functional issues.”

The changes include requiring developers to run games through Steam for at least five minutes. “Believe it or not, sometimes we review builds that have not ever been played through Steam,” Valve says. “This means that even if a game runs smoothly when a dev launches their executable directly, they might have missed basic steps that would impact a user installing and playing via Steam.”

Other requirements include making sure that achievements are ready and confirming any required common redistributables, like DirectX or Visual C++, that Steam may need to install.

Valve also says more improvements are in the works for the build review process.

BYD’s flagship luxury EV with 1,000+ km of range hits 4,000 sales in just 19 days

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

BYD wasted no time, with sales of its new flagship electric sedan reaching 4,000 in just 19 days since its launch.

With a record of more than 300,000 electric vehicles sold last month, BYD sold more EVs than the US did in the entire third quarter, combined.

A big part of the higher demand is thanks to BYD’s latest breakthrough technology, including its second-generation Blade Batteries and Flash Charging technology.

BYD began equipping vehicles with the new battery and charging tech in March, and over the past six months it has posted higher year-over-year sales growth. Through October, BYD sold 1,953,700 vehicles, up 6% compared to the same period in 2025.

On October 13, BYD launched the Great Han, or “Da Han” in China, the first fully electric D-segment flagship sedan in its Dynasty lineup.

Just 19 days after its launch, BYD said Great Han EV sales reached 4,000 units. At that rate, sales could reach 10,000 or more in its first full month.

With “class-beating high value,” BYD said, “it has firmly established itself in the mid-to-high-end sedan segment above 250,000 yuan,” which is about $37,500.

The Great Han is available in three variants, priced from 235,900 yuan, or about $35,000. Buyers can choose from single- or dual-motor powertrains. The standard rear-wheel-drive (RWD) Premium trim offers up to 1,008 km (626 miles) of CLTC range, while the all-wheel-drive (AWD) Flagship variant offers up to 880 km (546 miles).

All models use a 102.326 kWh BYD Blade 2.0 battery pack. The RWD models have a single 370 kW (496 hp) motor, while the AWD variant adds a 200 kW (268 hp) front motor for a combined 570 kW (764 hp).

Like most of its new vehicles, the Great Han has a roof-mounted LiDAR and BYD’s God’s Eye B, a more advanced version of its intelligent driving system.

The ADAS system offers safety and convenience features, including Navigate on Autopilot on highways and in cities, plus remote autonomous parking.

All variants also include premium features, such as BYD’s DiSus-A dual-chamber air suspension system, rear-wheel steering, and zero-gravity front seats.

Measuring 5,256 mm in length, 1,999 mm in width, and 1,510 mm in height, with a 3,130 mm wheelbase, the Great Han is slightly bigger than the Tesla Model S and Lucid Air. It’s closer in size to the long-wheelbase Mercedes-Benz S-Class.

Thanks to BYD’s second-gen Blade Batteries and Flash Charging technology, the vehicle’s battery can charge from 10% to 70% in just 5 minutes. A full recharge (10% to 97%) takes just 9 minutes.

The Great Han joins the Great Tang SUV as the flagship sedan under BYD’s Dynasty lineup. BYD announced that Great Tang sales reached 14,000 units in October, marking four months of sales above 10,000.

There might be hope for Arkane after all: A new report says multiple companies have made offers for it.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A new report from Bloomberg says Dishonored developer Arkane might not be as doomed as it appeared last month, as multiple companies have made offers to acquire the studio.

Citing "people familiar with the negotiations," the Bloomberg report says Ubisoft is among Arkane's suitors, as are at least two other unnamed gaming companies. Xbox has also considered selling the studio to its own management, effectively making it independent, although whether that option is still on the table isn't clear.

Despite producing an enviable slate of acclaimed immersive sims over the years (until it was compelled by senior leadership to do otherwise), Arkane fell victim to the Xbox "reset" in July that saw thousands of employees laid off (with more still awaiting their fates) and four studios cast adrift. Arkane avoided being the fifth, reportedly thanks to France's "Florange Law" that compelled Xbox to search for a buyer before pulling the plug.

The deadline for that search was November 13, after which Arkane would be shuttered—but a few weeks ahead of that date, Microsoft extended it by a month, to "explore options, including finding a partner to support the studio through the development work still ahead," a reference to the Marvel's Blade game Arkane currently has in development. That extension suggested there was at least some hope that Arkane might find a new home, instead of being closed.

Going from Xbox to Ubisoft could be viewed as a "frying pan to fire" move, given Ubisoft's own ongoing financial struggles, layoffs, and closures. But Arkane's immersive sim expertise could be a good fit for some of Ubisoft's biggest properties, including Assassin's Creed, Far Cry, and—yes, I'm just going to say it—Splinter Cell, which would be magnificent in the style of Dishonored. Arkane and Ubisoft both being French companies can't hurt, either.

I've reached out to Xbox and Ubisoft for comment on the report, and will update if I receive a reply.

SEC division views Canton Coin as digital commodity in no-action letter

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

a16z partner Miles Jennings welcomed the SEC staff's position, arguing it could signal a broader regulatory path for blockchain network tokens.

The U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission's Division of Corporation Finance said it views Canton Coin (CC) as a digital commodity and will not recommend enforcement action over its programmatic issuance under certain conditions.

In a no-action letter published Tuesday, the division said Canton Coin derives its value from the programmatic operation of the Canton Network and supply-and-demand dynamics, rather than expectations of profit from the managerial efforts of others. It also cited the network's lack of a central party.

"Based on the facts presented, the Division will not recommend enforcement action to the Commission if, in reliance on your opinion as counsel, Canton Coin is minted programmatically by Super Validators, Validators and application providers, in the manner and under the circumstances described in your letter, without registration under Section 5 of the Securities Act or Section 12(g) of the Exchange Act," Deputy Chief Counsel Jonathan Ingram wrote.

The letter reflects the Division of Corporation Finance's enforcement position based on the specific facts and representations submitted by Canton and does not express a legal conclusion on the issues presented, including whether Canton Coin is a security.

Canton Network is a blockchain infrastructure protocol developed by Digital Asset that aims to enable secure, privacy-enabled, and interoperable transactions across institutions and networks.

In May, 21Shares became the first U.S. ETF issuer to offer direct exposure to CC, the network's native utility token.

In June, Digital Asset raised $355 million in a funding round led by Andreessen Horowitz's a16z crypto fund to expand the Canton ecosystem and accelerate institutional adoption of its blockchain infrastructure.

a16z partner Miles Jennings welcomed the SEC staff's position, arguing it could signal a broader regulatory path for blockchain network tokens.

"Incredible. @CantonNetwork just received no action relief from the SEC for its network token," Jennings wrote Tuesday on X. "The @SECGov's token taxonomy from March and follow up FAQs have now created a clear pathway out of securities laws—Network tokens are digital commodities."

The Clarks Wallabee Goes Gorpcore

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Clarks has been on one lately. Over the past few weeks, Moncler worked up the British footwear brand's signature Wallabee, while A.P.C. took on the Desert Boot and the Weaver. Now, Clarks has called in Italian shoemaker Diemme to drag three of its biggest silhouettes up a mountain.

(In case you don't spend all day thinking about Italian hiking boots, here's a primer: Diemme was founded in Montebelluna, a town in northern Italy with a very serious footwear-making history. The brand is best known for taking the kinda boots originally built for mountains and making them nice enough to wear with your very best trousers. Every pair is still made at its factory in Veneto, and the upcoming Clarks collection is, too.)

The main event is the Wallabee, of course. Diemme reworked the familiar moccasin-shaped shoe and covered it in pale “champagne” suede from Charles F. Stead, the British tannery responsible for some of the nicest fuzzy leather in footwear. So far, so classic. But then you get the thick rope laces and rubber mudguard wrapped around the bottom. Underneath, the OG crepe sole has been swapped out for the chunky, lugged unit found on Diemme's Roccia Vet and Cornaro boots. It makes the whole shoe sit higher and gives it enough tread to deal with more than a slightly sticky pub floor. Your ’Bees are now ready to hit the trails.

There are two more styles in the cross-branded collection. The Desert Boot comes in the classic “cola” brown suede, only now it's been fitted with a heavy-duty sole. Then there's the Desert Trek, which has been transformed into a mid-cut boot and made from black full-grain leather. It keeps the signature center seam and little Trek Man logo, but it's easily the biggest and baddest of the three, with metal D-rings running up the lacing system and a beefed up ankle for extra support.

The Diemme x Clarks collection is set to drop on November 5, purchasable through Diemme, Clarks, and select stockists globally.

A version of this story originally appeared in British GQ.
