Mason Thames is set to voice Dick Grayson

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The rumors are true: Mason Thames is indeed set to voice Dick Grayson aka Robin in DC's upcoming animated Dynamic Duo movie.

"We always talk about wanting volunteers and not recruits to be part of the DCU, to be part of DC Studios, and Mason always wanted to be Dick Grayson. He just did," DC Studios co-CEO Peter Safran told Collider. "And he came in, and auditioned the s*** out of it, and was awesome. He's a wonderful young actor, but boy, did he want this. He was just obsessed. When he came over to the DC offices, he came over with his dad at the time, and we’ve got tons of great archives and stuff in there, and wanted to take a picture of everything and with everything. He's a fan."

Deadline previously reported that Mason Thames, best known for starring in The Black Phone and the live-action How to Train Your Dragon movie, was in talks to voice Grayson, with Ghostbusters: Afterlife's Logan Kim set to voice Jason Todd, the second hero to don the Robin moniker... though Safran has only confirmed Thames thus far. Steve Buscemi has reportedly been cast as Red Hood Two.

The animated movie is set to to tell the origin story of both Robins as the two friends "navigate their dreams and differing visions for the future." Dynamic Duo is not canon to the DCU, therefore a Robin (or a Batman for that matter) has still not been cast for the DCU.

The Batman director Matt Reeves' production company, 6th & Idaho is set to produce the film, with Arthur Mintz directing from a script by The script is written by Matthew Aldrich.

Dynamic Duo is set to release on September 22, 2028. For more, check out our guide on how to watch the DC movies in order, and keep up with new DC movies and shows on the way.

Colin Jost Takes Gift-Giving Seriously

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The Weekend Update co-host and Kay campaign star shares his approach to holiday shopping.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Finding the perfect holiday gift isn’t easy. Between the pressure to get it right, the endless wish lists, and the people who insist they don’t want anything, shopping for loved ones can feel like an impossible mission. Even Colin Jost, who has spent years navigating the pressures of live television, admits that the process is incredibly stressful.

This festive season, the Weekend Update co-host is putting his comedic talents to work with Kay by starring in the jeweler’s latest holiday campaign, “Love All In.” The cheeky ad follows Jost on an increasingly ridiculous quest to find the perfect present, poking fun at the lengths we’ll go to for the people closest to us. It’s a fitting partnership for someone who knows a thing or two about making people laugh but also takes gifting pretty seriously.

“I think I’m a good gift giver,” Jost tells ELLE. “Giving gifts is stressful because I do want it to be a meaningful gift. I hate the idea of just buying something because you have to give someone something.”

For Jost, the secret to getting it right has less to do with how much you spend and more to do with how much thought goes into it. “At every kind of price point for any gift, there [are] thoughtful and unthoughtful versions,” he says. “Something can be really expensive but not thoughtful.”

It’s a sentiment that aligns with Kay’s latest campaign, which encourages shoppers to go “all in” when expressing their love. And while Jost might not be the first person you’d think to turn to for jewelry advice, he’s developed an appreciation for it over the years, thanks to his very chic wife, Scarlett Johansson.

“Until five years ago, I didn’t own a watch,” he admits. That changed when the actress gifted him his first one, introducing him to an entirely new world of accessories. As for whether she’s influenced his personal style? “In that it’s exclusively managed by her, yes, I would say she’s influenced it.”

Despite his Staten Island roots, Jost’s jewelry preferences are surprisingly understated. “I don’t really wear a ton of jewelry, but I also am from Staten Island, so I do have to have certain pieces, or else they won’t let me back over the Verrazano Bridge,” he jokes.

While browsing Kay’s assortment, he found himself drawn to simple gold pieces, including a ring, bracelet, and necklace. “They were really nice, but also they weren’t gaudy or anything,” he says. His preference for gold also comes with some rather unconventional reasoning: “If we get into a post-apocalyptic situation, it feels like it’s better to melt down for trading.”

When it comes to shopping for the people closest to him, Jost admits that both Johansson and his Weekend Update collaborator Michael Che are difficult to buy things for, though he found a creative solution for the latter. “I got Che a really large and heavy, realistic statue of a velociraptor,” he recalls. “It was so heavy that he literally can’t get rid of it.” The real expense wasn’t the dinosaur itself. “The main cost was shipping,” he says. “It was a huge eyesore, and it’s prohibitively expensive for him to have removed from his property.”

The hardest person to gift on Jost’s list? His father, who insists he doesn’t need anything. “I think he actually means it, but it’s so unhelpful,” Jost says. After years of nostalgic gifts inspired by his dad’s past, he’s running out of ideas. “I’ve got to figure out something that breaks through his defenses.”

In terms of gifting etiquette, Jost has a few rules of his own. For starters, he’s big on surprises. While he understands the appeal of a wish list for children, he thinks adults should leave a little room for the unexpected. “For adults, it’s got to be ‘surprise me,’” he says. “The surprise is half the fun, three-quarters of the fun.” Regifting, on the other hand, comes with a few conditions. He’s open to the idea, but only if the gift is a perfect fit for someone and there’s full disclosure.

But perhaps the most surprising revelation comes when I ask what’s on his own holiday wish list. After spending much of our conversation discussing what to buy everyone else, Jost seems relieved to finally be on the receiving end. “Oh, thank you! Finally, someone asked me. What do I want?”

His answer? Nothing material. “At this point in life, I mostly want experiences,” he says. Whether it’s a trip or simply someone taking the initiative to organize an outing, he’s more interested in making memories than collecting more things. “Even if it’s just the plan of, ‘Let’s go do this at this time,’ I think that’s a great gift.” Or, when in doubt, a velociraptor.

I'm trying to imagine a Ubisoft logo on Dishonored 3

Ubisoft and at least two other companies have reportedly offered to buy Dishonored and Marvel's Blade dev Arkane Studios from Microsoft as the company seeks to spin out multiple Xbox developers.

Bloomberg reports that Ubisoft – which, like Arkane, is based in France – placed an offer in undisclosed and early-stage negotiations.

Other Xbox studios, like Psychonauts 2 maker Double Fine, have gone independent post-Xbox as Microsoft brute forces a "reset" plan under new leadership. Two agreements to save Hellblade developer Ninja Theory fell through, and in September, Arkane's future was also looking uncertain. Last month, in a move that sure seems to align with today's report, Xbox announced an extension to the deadline to "explore options" for Arkane's future.

Conflicting reports make it difficult to estimate how much work still needs to be done on Blade, which has occupied Arkane for years, including the years after Xbox closed down Arkane Austin following the Redfall disaster. Whether it's almost finished or still needs a year of work, Blade will need new backing, which is where this deal comes in.

Ubisoft would be a curious home for Arkane, though you could point to shared stealth action DNA between Dishonored and Assassin's Creed. And again, they're also both French, which is especially relevant concerning labor laws.

However, much like Microsoft, Ubisoft has a reputation for shuttering a lot of teams and cancelling a lot of games, though you'll hardly find a large publisher with a squeaky-clean graveyard these days.

Ubisoft is also going through its own reset following several disappointing years, with a new subsidiary dedicated to pillar franchises like Assassin's Creed propped up with backing from Tencent. By some miracle, this reset has not yet seen the end of Beyond Good & Evil 2, which – not to be outdone by the misguided Dragon Age: The Veilguard pivots that certainly contributed to the end of BioWare – Ubisoft apparently wanted to be a live service game at one point.

Fernando Calvillo, Beloved Fixture of Oklahoma's Art Community, Dies in Train Crash in Spain

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

“He embraced people wholeheartedly, recognized what they needed, and connected them with opportunities and with one another,” said Calvillo’s colleague.

Fernando Calvillo, an esteemed artist and leader in Oklahoma City's arts community, died in a high-speed collision between two trains in the province of Toledo, Spain, on Wednesday, October 28. He was aboard with his husband, Lawrence Naff, who is expected to recover from serious injuries at a local hospital.

The crash, which Spanish Transport Minister Óscar Puente attributed to computational failure and human oversight, claimed the lives of at least 68 other people and comes just nine months after another crash in southern Andalusia in Spain, which claimed the lives of 46 people. Puente resigned today, November 3, after a congressional session regarding rail safety.

Calvillo was declared a missing person until the Mexican Foreign Ministry and the Oklahoma Visual Arts Coalition (OVAC), where Calvillo worked as the director of community relations, confirmed his death in separate statements on Monday, November 1.

$12,000 Buffalo–New York Fares Making Headlines—I’m Finding Nonstops For $337 Roundtrip

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

A $12,203 airfare between Buffalo and New York about 300 miles each way – is going viral as evidence of how expensive it’s gotten to fly home for the holidays. The TikTok video that started it off though shows a roundtrip price (not one-way) and connecting both directions in Florida (not non-stop). Meanwhile, nonstops on that route seem to start at $337 roundtrip. Kristen Mirand says she was trying to get home to Buffalo and received a notification for a $200 flight from New York. Then she looked at returning, and found five-figure prices with connections in Orlando and Fort Lauderdale. She joked that she’d start walking. The New York Post ran with “Flight from Buffalo to NYC reportedly reached $12K — as holiday airfare hits an all-time high” and suggests higher fuel costs and holiday demand are involved here. ZeroHedge describes a “roughly 90 minute flight,” and launches into airlines keeping fares high after fuel prices fall. It ends with the prospect of airlines “laughing all the way to the bank.” Look at the flights actually displayed in the video: $11,400 roundtrip, returning through Orlando. These are 7 and 9 hour trips from Buffalo to JFK. $12,203 roundtrip, returning through Fort Lauderdale. These are 7 and 8.5 hour trips. Every price is labeled round trip. you’re seeing the cost of the whole ticket when you select a return. This wasn’t a $12,000 nonstop and none of these itineraries gets you there in 90 minutes. I’m seeing nonstop roundtrips around $337 on Delta Air Lines, JetBlue, United Airlines and American Airlines, checking across the New York airports. Even if you want to connect in Florida, Frontier Airlines can be as low as $210 roundtrip. She doesn’t show her dates, but at those prices be flexible! My guess is that the pricing is breaking somewhere as the system puts together this unnatural itinerary. A fare between Buffalo and New York comes with rules about how you can travel and Florida connections aren’t usually included in the routing rules. A search can wind up combining separate fares for different pieces of the trip instead of using the simple fare between the two cities. Bad fare combining or an error putting together the outbound and return can cause weird outcomes. While the last seats on a flight can be expensive, “last seat available” doesn’t seem like the explanation for a $12,000 Buffalo–New York price. Airfares are up. Hopper’s says Thanksgiving domestic roundtrips average $402, up 31%, and Christmas $452, up 23%. With millions of fare quotes being generated, even a tiny error rate is going to produce outlier results. Mistake fares can be fares that are too high, not just too low. Faced with a fare like this, I’d: Search for nonstops on the date I actually want. Start with the trip I’d choose to take before considering a seven-hour detour. Check JFK, LaGuardia and Newark. Restricting the search to one airport leaves out alternatives that may work just as well. Search the airline websites directly. See whether the airline produces the same price, rather than treating one Google Flights result as the answer. Start a fresh search and compare one-ways with the roundtrip. Check the dates, cabin, passenger count and currency. Separate searches can get you away from whatever combination generated the nonsense price. For this route I’d also price Amtrak’s Empire Service between Buffalo and New York. Those Florida connections already consume most of the day before you add getting to the airport, security and the trip into Manhattan. North Korean fake job interview led to $6.3B Binance heist

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

'It was not a single isolated breach or a simple phishing incident,' exchange says

North Korean cryptocurrency thieves tricked a Binance software engineer into downloading malware onto their work-issued computer during a fake job interview process before last week's record-breaking $6.3 billion heist.

In an incident report published on Tuesday, the cryptocurrency exchange said several techniques - including social engineering via fake job interviews, malware downloads, compromised internal AI tooling, endpoint takeover, and an “advanced transaction-authorization method” - led to the October 29 security breach.

“Our current investigation indicates that this was a sophisticated, multi-stage campaign,” the incident report said. “It was not a single isolated breach or a simple phishing incident.”

Over the weekend, the FBI attributed the theft to TraderTraitor, a North Korean hacking operation linked to Lazarus Group, known for combining espionage with massive cryptocurrency heists. The Pyongyang-backed crew is also highly adept at job market scams - both posing as IT workers, who are then hired by Western companies, and as potential employers to lure software engineers into downloading malware and infecting corporate devices.

With the recent Binance attack, the Norks used the latter.

The attack prep began back in June with a supply chain attack when one of its engineers downloaded a malicious Homebrew installation. While the malware was detected by the security team, the device was replaced, and all affected credentials rotated, “Engineer A’s role and his related colleagues on our wallet infrastructure team was exposed to the hacker,” the report says.

Three months later, the social engineering part of the attack happened.

In September, another software engineer was contacted via a “legitimate job-hunting platform” by someone purporting to be a project director who arranged a series of job interviews for the Binance engineer with the fake company. During the first round - a live video interview with the engineer and a fake HR rep - the interviewer “shared a social media account with him, including personal photos and videos posted on the platform, in order to build trust,” according to the report. “This was more advanced and deceptive than previously seen social engineering approaches.”

The second interview with someone purporting to be the CEO of the phony company was conducted on a “popular social chat app,” and toward the end, the CEO instructed the engineer to download an “assignment” containing a malware-laced code repository. The code executed on his work computer during the setup process, which kicked off a series of unauthorized activities.

According to Binance, the malware replaced a legitimate development tool component with a malicious version, which executed commands and established communication with an attacker-controlled external server. The attackers also took steps to hide the malicious script and evade detection, and it worked. Binance’s endpoint security tools did not immediately detect the malware.

CREATE  /opt/homebrew/Cellar/pyenv/2.8.0/libexec/pyenv_

DELETE  .../libexec/pyenv  (3,344 B)      ← remove the valid pyenv

RENAME  pyenv_ → pyenv  (3,414 B)  ← replace it with a malicious version

DELETE  /opt/homebrew/bin/pyenv  (3,414 B)

Then, on October 20, the miscreants used the compromised computer and an internal AI tool to gain access to the computers of wallet DevOps engineer A and another wallet software developer, Engineer C.

Engineer A had a container deployment permission token on his computer, which the Norks soon discovered. On October 29, the attacker accessed Engineer C's computer, used the stolen credentials from Engineer A to deploy a container, and triggered a script to launch the cryptocurrency-stealing attack.

As of Tuesday, Binance said it has rebuilt the compromised wallet system and internal AI tool, and replaced all of the affected devices. It also decommissioned all credentials and tokens assigned to the compromised engineers, and is working with law enforcement, blockchain intel firms, and security researchers to trace and freeze the assets.

The crypto exchange has also hired independent cybersecurity experts to conduct a cybersecurity review of the incident, and recommend additional protective measures. Binance said it’s also working to establish a security research fund and initiatives with AI companies and security shops to focus on AI security in blockchain environments.®

The 7 Best Nail Trends to Try Right Now

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

From “Winter Wine” to “Tiger’s Eye,” these looks are perfect for colder months ahead

It’s a little too early for holiday nail art manicures, and maybe even too late for a fall palette of maples, golds, and pumpkin polish shades. So, what to do in the weeks of November ahead when it comes to your nail look?

“November calls for rich, warming shades such as chocolate brown, mocha, espresso, and caramel, alongside deep cherry, oxblood, and olive green,” says Townhouse founder Juanita Huber-Millet. “Our Flawless collection captures the season beautifully, with coffee-inspired shades like Chai Latte and Mocha, and timeless reds such as Red Apple and Cherry for an effortlessly chic fall manicure.”

Huber-Millet also recommends burgundy, classic red, and deep plum for a timeless winter shade. “This season, expect modern twists with glossy chrome and cat-eye finishes, colored French tips and delicate gems that add a festive touch without compromising on sophistication,” the professional adds.

Below, discover our edit of the best nail trends and colors to wear this November and beyond, including gemstone-inspired cat-eye shades and modern, high-shine neutrals to suit any occasion.

A rich, high-shine black-brown shade is the new classic color for the season. The key is to opt out of milkier shades and lean into depth, with crisp, clean cuticles and a generously glossy top coat.

Dress up purple and burgundy shades with subtle polka dots, embracing the year’s most playful nail art trend with a sophisticated, grown-up edge. The key is to keep the dots spacious and minimal.

A clean, black manicure is as much a timeless winter staple as a gray layering piece or a pair of leather boots. The key is to keep your nails short and smart with a soft-square shape, and pay extra attention to hydrating and tidying your cuticles.

Trade cow print and leopard spots for a more elevated, minimal zebra print this November. The best method is to put a playful twist on the classic French tip manicure, using fine lines to create subtle definition.

November’s take on the burgundy manicure is the darkest, glossiest yet. Pair deep, shiny pinotage tones with a classic shape, like almond or soft-square, and embrace purple-leaning hues over classic reds.

“Chrome and cat-eye finishes are key trends this winter,” says Huber-Millet. “At Townhouse, we’re seeing brown chrome become a firm favorite, combining rich, chocolatey tones with a luminous finish for a manicure that feels luxurious, contemporary, and perfectly suited to the season.”

The result? A multidimensional finish evocative of the classic tiger’s-eye gemstone.

Skip natural pink chrome and cat-eye this season and embrace rich, dark bases, from black to brown to burgundy. For a wintry twist, opt for a reflective blue or silver sheer chrome that allows the depth to shine through while highlighting its unique shift.

Anthropic’s Claude AI Predicts a Massive BTC USD Move

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

Bitcoin (BTC) might not need another halving, another wave of institutional adoption, or even another round of monetary easing to deliver one of its most explosive rallies yet. Anthropic’s Claude AI predicts that it might need something considerably bigger: a fundamental shift in how the world’s largest economies treat Bitcoin.

Imagine the United States announcing a landmark expansion of its strategic Bitcoin reserve, followed by several major sovereign wealth funds committing billions of dollars to BTC.

Add a deterioration in confidence in traditional government debt and a sudden rush towards alternative stores of value, and Bitcoin could find itself at the center of an extraordinary financial realignment.

The most explosive scenario would begin with Washington committing to actively accumulate Bitcoin rather than simply retaining its existing seized holdings. An official purchasing program, potentially paired with incentives for allied nations to build their own reserves, could fundamentally change Bitcoin’s investment narrative.

The potential domino effect would be enormous:

Government adoption: The US establishes a long-term Bitcoin accumulation strategy, encouraging other nations to follow.

Sovereign wealth funds: Major investment funds in the Middle East and Asia begin allocating even small percentages of their portfolios to BTC.

Institutional FOMO: Pension funds, insurance companies, and asset managers accelerate allocations to avoid being left behind.

Monetary easing: Deteriorating government debt markets force central banks towards looser monetary policy, potentially increasing demand for scarce assets.

Bitcoin’s fixed supply of 21 million coins makes this particularly interesting. With approximately 450 new BTC entering circulation daily, a sudden acceleration in institutional and sovereign demand could create a substantial supply-demand imbalance.

From a technical perspective, Bitcoin would first need to reclaim the $80,000-$85,000 region and establish it as support. A sustained breakout above $90,000 would provide a much stronger bullish signal, potentially attracting momentum traders and sidelined institutional capital.

Beyond that, the $100,000 psychological milestone would become the next major battleground. A convincing weekly breakout, accompanied by expanding trading volume, a bullish MACD crossover, and RSI momentum returning to stronger territory, could provide the foundations for another major price discovery phase.

If institutional inflows accelerated alongside these developments, Bitcoin could potentially target $150,000 before entering an even more aggressive speculative rally.

The final leg towards $250,000 would require an extraordinary combination of sovereign adoption, institutional FOMO, and favorable macroeconomic conditions. This is not a technical outcome we can currently assume; it would depend heavily on the hypothetical catalyst materializing.

What sets Bitcoin Hyper apart is its unique blend of a meme-driven community and serious infrastructure. The project features a Decentralized Canonical Bridge for seamless Bitcoin transfers, along with a high-speed Layer 2 designed for efficient, low-latency performance.

In summary, Bitcoin Hyper aims to unlock a new era of Bitcoin utility and scalability. Momentum is building rapidly. The team recently launched the official Bitcoin Hyper Wallet, which is now open for community testing. This follows the successful rollout of the Bitcoin Hyper Testnet and Explorer.

That would be a monumental rally from current levels, but in a hypothetical world where sovereign governments compete to accumulate Bitcoin, that figure might not seem so ridiculous.

Opinion: 123 games later, Liverpool star's time is up and Fabrizio Romano update confirms it

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

One Liverpool star's time at Anfield is up. The Reds have a replacement lined up and there's surely no way back from here.

Liverpool will continue to reshape their squad in 2027. That much is for sure. We imagine they were always going to do that but Richard Hughes leaving the sporting director role just confirms it.

The Reds did make a sensible move of promoting Julian Ward into the position, making sure there's continuity there with the long-term planning. But at the same time, this squad build hasn't been a good one and work has to be done in order to balance things.

That's the case all over the squad, honestly. Liverpool look imbalanced in defence, midfield and attack - and that's before considering the fact that half the squad face uncertain long-term future because of their contracts.

Some players look absolutely certain to leave, in fact, and one of those will likely bring an end to his time at Anfield after over 100 appearances. We don't see any realistic way back.

Kostas Tsimikas's uncertain futureLiverpool were completely open to selling Kostas Tsimikas in 2025 but a solution couldn't be found that suited all parties. As it turns out, the Reds were fortunate that a deal didn't go through because Andy Robertson's departure created a place they needed to fill. Having Tsimikas backup Milos Kerkez was the best solution. Buying someone would have created a further complication in what was already a complicated summer. But it looks absolutely certain that this will be Tsimikas's final season. After 123 matches and counting, the scene is set for his departure once his contract expires at the end of the campaign.

Having Tsimikas backup Milos Kerkez was the best solution. Buying someone would have created a further complication in what was already a complicated summer.

But it looks absolutely certain that this will be Tsimikas's final season. After 123 matches and counting, the scene is set for his departure once his contract expires at the end of the campaign.

We do expect that to be the case anyway but it looks even more certain now that Alejandro Balde is available from Barcelona. That's the line from Fabrizio Romano, anyway, who claims the left-back is considering his future after limited appearances this term.

Liverpool were interested in a deal back in the summer and journalist Juan Jesus claims that interest remains. All that was missing was Balde actively wanting to depart Barcelona - and the tide has turned there, it seems.

Which effectively calls time on Tsimikas's Anfield career. Balde would be an ideal replacement as a young defender with top-level experience and a playing style that Liverpool actively want.

It's difficult to see any way back for the Greek Scouser. Perhaps a fantastic second half of the season changes things but even then, we expect Liverpool will look to invest in Balde.

F1 Academy Driver Emma Felbermayr and Audi Reveal the Ultimate Way to Experience Race Weekend in Austin

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

In their first-ever weekend at the United States Grand Prix, the latest F1 team to join the grid celebrated in style.

Before Miami or Las Vegas, the United States Grand Prix in Austin, Texas, was the only race in the U.S. for Formula 1. And it has quite the history! The iconic Circuit of the Americas track was purposefully built for the sport and has become a quick favorite amongst drivers and fans alike thanks to its stunning turns that were inspired by the likes of the Silverstone Circuit in the United Kingdom and Bahrain’s International Circuit and spacing that allows for fun overturns and racing to happen at all parts of the circuit. (Try overtaking during a street race, we dare you!) But as a person who has experienced several races in her lifetime, the vibes of Austin stood out to me as the iconic venue brought the sport back to the Lone Star State once again for another incredible race that sets itself apart by being clear that it’s made by the fans and for the fans. And the mood was only elevated by the introduction of some brand-new teams in the circuit including the Audi Revolut F1 Team.

In a surprise twist, this year they were also joined by F1 Academy after a schedule change moved one of their planned three-race weekends over to Austin. Which also meant that for the first time, second-year driver Emma Felbermayr would be experiencing the track for herself. And as someone who was heading to the United States Grand Prix weekend for the first time alongside Audi, it felt like fate that we were going to have this new adventure together!

To get the epic Audi experience, the team invited us out to the 2026 United States Grand Prix to see race weekend up close. And in true Texas fashion, we got to see firsthand the real meaning of Southern hospitality. Get ready to find out how Audi is not only bringing a new kind of venture to Austin, but also how Emma got ready for an unexpected weekend in the Lone Star State.

(P.S. The writer received free stays, meals, and experiences in order to write this story.)

I was determined to finally get to witness an entire full race weekend for the first time (third time is the charm, after all), so I traveled out the day before so I wouldn’t have to miss a single second. Ironically, this has also been part of Emma’s plan for success this year.

“This year I took myself a bit more time to see the cities. Last year I was still in school. I always arrived the day before in the evening and went back on Sunday,” she revealed to Cosmopolitan after her final race of the weekend. “But this year, I arrive one day earlier before media day starts to do things you have to do in that specific city.”

Emma used the extra time to her advantage, saying that she went to the city to “try cowboy boots, hats, and all that stuff. But also the most important is getting used to the time zone.”

In a special surprise, Audi allowed me to bring a +1 and so I brought my dad out for his first official race. We hadn’t even made it to the track yet when he let out his first, “Oh my god,” as we were greeted by an Audi representative and were shuffled into a brand-new 2027 Audi Q9, our vehicle for the weekend. Our driver, Cody, let us know that the vehicle only had 18 miles on it before he picked it up, meaning we were truly one of the first to step into it.

Unfortunately, since we got in a bit late, we couldn’t really follow in Emma’s footsteps and take in the sights. Instead, our only stop after our flight was our hotel, the newly opened 1 Hotel Austin. The hotel has a strong link to Audi (more on that later) and we automatically felt right at home as we walked in and immediately headed up to our room.

Room service was absolutely necessary and I totally recommend their wagyu burger which also came with some perfectly cooked fries. After a quick shower, we settled in for the night as we got ready for an incredibly fun-filled weekend.

Practice day meant a more chill day at COTA and a later start than the following few days, so I was able to kick things off with a massage at the Bamford Wellness Spa in 1 Hotel Austin. Not only is there a sauna that I wish I had more time to enjoy, but the staff there were one in a million and made me feel so welcomed and relaxed. The deep tissue massage was a welcome reset after a flight and it got me so energized for an exciting first day.

Unlike in Formula 1, where drivers get three official practices before the qualifying session, Formula 1 Academy drivers already come into day one ready to participate in their first of three races of the weekend. While Emma notes that simulator training does help them be fully prepared for packed weekends like this one, nothing relates to the real thing.

“The track time you get isn't a lot. In karting you don't really learn that you have to prepare yourself that much for a weekend, because you've got five test sessions before every weekend. But here you only have one, so that's the only session you can try something on track and just go through different situations before a weekend,” Emma said.

And so, it was important for us to get the lay of the land. As my dad and I got to the track in the middle of the day and it was officially time to take everything in. We did a hot girl walk around the track going from Turn 1—where he would be taking in the race all weekend—before walking backwards the track’s direction until we hit turn 16. It allowed us to get a pretty good idea of what the track was like and also check out some of the incredible spots where other folks who were attending were able to see the races.

It was a shorter day at COTA, but for a good reason. We ran back to the hotel after doing some very important shopping at the F1 merch shops and got in just in time for our special dinner experience, “Driven by the Journey,” a special partnership between Audi, 1 Hotels, and SingleThread—a three-star Michelin restaurant located in Sonoma County in California.

The dinner brought the iconic SingleThread Farms experience to Austin with a special five-course menu created by owner-chef Kyle Connaughton that featured fresh produce picked from the farm and picked out by owner-farmer Katina Connaughton. Each course also came with a wine pairing and it was truly one of the most incredible dining journeys I’ve ever had the pleasure to try. And the views from the hotel’s 14th floor were absolutely gorgeous, especially as we caught the Austin sunset.

As the sun went down, we went straight up to the rooftop to Watr—1 Hotel’s Japanese-inspired restaurant—where the night was just getting started for the ultimate nightcap.

It was a very full day on the track, so we headed out first thing in the morning in order to catch every single minute. Somehow, Audi worked their magic and gave my dad the greatest gifts you can give an F1 fan: a Paddock Club pass. Some lucky fans are able to win the chance to go into the Paddock Club with a team, while a few tickets are also sold to the public, but usually it’s access that’s reserved for those who are close to the team like sponsors or friends and family.

After catching up with the rest of the group, we walked over to the pit lane to get a private tour of the Audi F1 garage. Not only did we get to see the cars up close, but we also got to see the incredible mechanics and engineers get ready for qualifying day.

We also caught Emma’s Q&A back in the Paddock Club, where she shared her experience so far in Austin and her hope for the rest of the weekend as she prepared for her second race.

“The track is so wide, so big, and with our cars being that small compared to F1 cars, it it's even bigger. So I'm enjoying it a lot,” she revealed.

Soon after, we were heading out to the track for the ultimate experience: a Pirelli Hot Lap. Since I was the first one up for our session, I ran straight into the special Audi RS 5 where Audi F1 ambassador and current F1 Academy mentor Carrie Schreiner took me for the ultimate ride around the track that went up to 150 miles per hour. Just when I thought that walking around COTA was the best experience, there’s nothing like actually getting driven around the track.

Then came the day’s biggest main events: the F1 qualifying race as well as F1 Academy’s Reverse Grid Race. Grabbing one of the team radios only available in the Paddock Club and in the garage, we were able to listen to the team’s communications that made for a fun and different experience compared to watching at home.

For dinner, we headed out into the town and checked out Comedor, a spectacular Mexican restaurant located in downtown Austin where we grabbed some guacamole topped with chapulines—or Mexican crickets—and some Crab Luis Totopos that I will be dreaming about for years.

Then it was back to 1 Hotel for a much-needed rest before the final day. Emma notes that relaxing and getting a good night’s sleep before a race day is always key.

“I like to get my nine hours every day. I just like getting into bed early and maybe watch something on Netflix, not doing so much,” she said. What’s she watching right now? Outer Banks and some reality TV, along with Gossip Girl as she prepares for her first trip to NYC with Formula 1 Academy. But no time for an all-nighter! There’s still race day to look forward to!

It was finally race day and things couldn’t be more heated as both Formula 1 and Formula 1 Academy geared up for another wild day.

After grabbing a smoothie at Neighbors Cafe at 1 Hotel, my dad and I got one final look around the track.

Bonus: There’s one new experience at the United States Grand Prix that blew my mind during race day: the Guest House at Turn 20. In partnership with Austin’s Guest House restaurant, the special private hospitality space gives the perfect view of the final turn into the pit lane and includes a gourmet menu, fun performances throughout the day, and a bar with specialty drinks. Believe me, if you want to get as close to the Paddock Club experience, this is it!

We got to the track just in time to see Emma in her final race of the weekend which left her third in the overall Formula 1 Academy standings. There’s only one more race left in the F1A calendar: Las Vegas.

“It's going to be so much colder, so it's going to be different. Since I've raced there already last year, that's at least an advantage for myself compared to this weekend,” she added. “I think I just can focus a bit more on the driving through practice than learning the track.”

Emma is looking forward to resetting herself before then, something she says is key to further success.

“Either after a bad or a good weekend, you need to reset. If it was a good weekend, it doesn't mean the next one is going to be good as well. But if it's a bad one, there is a chance to come back stronger.”

Part of that includes hanging out with friends and family while she’s back home.

“I love seeing my friends. Since we are traveling so much, I don't get to see them really often. But when I do, it's even more nice,” Emma revealed.

She hasn’t revealed her plans for next season, especially as she finishes up her two-year limit in Formula 1 Academy.

“I'd say it's a bit hard to say now. I don't have proper plans for next year, so we are a bit on seeing the options,” she noted.

But, above all, she’s keeping one thing in mind: her love of racing.

“It was always about having fun, enjoying it, and that's also why I do it. I've always done what I really enjoyed doing and what I felt was the right thing to do.”

And so, with one final look at COTA and a last “yee-haw,” we officially said goodbye to the United States Grand Prix and Audi’s first rodeo in Austin. If this is what their first go-around is like, we can’t wait to witness what they have coming up next!

Raphinha underlines Barcelona’s class to give Aston Villa a reality check

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

This was a chastening night for Aston Villa, and a familiar one for Unai Emery in this corner of town. On a processional night at the Camp Nou Barcelona produced a performance of unhurried, high-grade incision, easing to a 3-0 Champions League victory that never really seemed to tip into an actual close-quarter contest before it was already gone.

“There are a lot of things to learn today,” Emery said afterwards. “Overall we are competing fantastically in the Champions League; we have nine points. Today, we saw there is still more to do to get to this level. Even sometimes in this match we were close to achieving it. We had chances, but we must be ruthless in the final third. Three-nil maybe was more than we deserved.”

This Barcelona team will always give you a chance, a thrilling mix of high-spec possession football and large open spaces behind their own teasingly high line. But Villa never looked like getting their teeth into those tender spots, always held at arm’s length, fists whirling in the space between.

Two goals here for Raphinha maintained his numerically superlative start to the season. But Pedri was the real star, producing 69 minutes of elite midfield guile, a footballer who at times like these seems to be tickling you very gently to sleep while someone else sticks the dagger in.

This was a significant game for its emotional weight, rather than any real sporting jeopardy at this early meandering stage in the Champions League’s expanded format. For the Villa fans crammed into their pen at the top of the stand it was a chance to register a note of progress for this high-wire, ever-evolving team just by taking part.

Currently the Camp Nou is in a state of mid-renovation chaos, packed with debris and random clutter, and with vast gushing holes in the raggedy corrugated roof. Think: Old Trafford, but deliberately like that. The new tiers at the top of the stands are already in place. The internal fixtures are being laid between hoardings and fences. Even on a drab and chilly November night you could already feel the outline of the coming mega-stadium.

But the atmosphere here was still a little thin at the start, the cagoule-clad home sections heavily speckled with empty seats. Hansi Flick put out a strong team here, with Lamine Yamal on the right and his hard-running straight-man Anthony Gordon on the opposite flank. Rodri and Pedri were the midfield pivot, a delicious-looking thing in outline, two physical extremes, the familiar wardrobe-like unit of high-spec midfield generalship, and next to him a more classically twirling, ball-funnelling Barça-issue sprite. Pedri is fun to watch even away from the ball, with that easy, fluid way of moving, just rolling along, never really seeming to stop or switch directions, like a paper bag being blown across the turf.

Paris Saint-Germain set a record as the quickest team to score four goals in the Champions League by powering into a 4-0 lead after 17 minutes against Villarreal on their way to a 6-0 win.

João Neves started the goal-blitz before Desiré Doué, Senny Mayulu and Fabián Ruiz quickly put the tie to bed. Ruiz struck his second to make it 5-0 soon after the interval and substitute David Boly completed the scoring as PSG extended their 100%  Champions League start and moved three points clear at the top of the table.

Since taking just two points from their first three Ligue 1 games this season, which included a 2-1 home defeat to Monaco, PSG have now won 10 successive games in all competitions.

Inter were equally convincing, beating Feyenoord 3-0 at De Kuip. Yann Bisseck curled in the opener from long range, Pio Esposito headed the second before ⁠half-time and Piotr Zielinski completed the scoring from ​distance after ​the restart.

Sporting Lisbon beat Shakhtar Donetsk 1-0, a day after parting ways with their head coach, Rui Borges.  Under the reserve coach, Tiago Fernandes, the Portuguese giants picked up their third win in the Champions League this season, with Issa Doumbia scoring the decisive goal in the game at Stamford Bridge.

Aleksey Batrakov scored two goals in quick succession to seal a 2-0 win for Galatasaray against Stuttgart. Batrakov opened the scoring in the 12th minute at Rams Park and struck again three minutes later. Austria's Lask won 4-0 against Slovan Bratislava and Bodø/Glimt drew 0-0 with Lille

Stadiums across Europe fell silent before kick-off in memory of the Tenerife player Jesús de Miguel, who died earlier on Tuesday after a collision on the pitch in the Spanish second division match against Andorra on Monday. Agencies

Emery picked a team to press and stifle the space. It was always likely to be the plan against these opponents, ideologically wed to their own version of the Cruyffian omni-ball possession game. But then, everyone has a plan until they get lulled into a state of early-onset mental fatigue chasing shirt numbers and empty spaces.

Villa sat deep and snapped into their challenges. There were a couple of long passes from John McGinn on the right to set Nicolas Jackson in beyond the Barcelona line. On the other side Lamine Yamal looked, as ever, like a different species of human every time he took the ball, just in the way he snaps and grooves inside, a player who walks on to every pitch carrying his own portable yard of space.

With seven minutes gone it was Lamine Yamal who opened the scoring, in a way that felt almost too easy and too natural, cutting in from the left, too quick in his movements, all alone in front of goal suddenly even in that crowded space, with time to clip a simple left-footed shot into the far corner.

The Camp Nou let out a devotional roar. Lamine Yamal ran through his brand-friendly celebration routine in front of the ultras behind the goal. And at that point you feared for Emery’s team on that open right side.

Villa did come surging back straight away, winning a succession of corners. Johan Manzambi shot wide after good work from Jackson. But with 12 minutes gone Ian Maatsen was booked for bringing down Lamine Yamal as he eased away by the touchline, a Chekhov’s shotgun-style scenario for any full-back given the nightmarish job of marking this miracle of feints and snaps and pond-skater glides.

Kick off your evenings with the Guardian's take on the world of football

Gordon had a shot well saved by Zion Suzuki after a disastrous attempt to play out from the back had been strangled by the Barcelona press. But the game wasn’t really changed by Barcelona going 1-0 up. When they play like this the test is as much patience, concentration and retaining the energy to break when chance arrives. It did finally on 33 minutes, Manzambi driving down the left before shooting too close to Joan García.

But half-time arrived with Barcelona looking a little too easy and unbloodied for their lead.

Rodri was withdrawn and replaced by 19-year-old Marc Bernal, another high-grade home-farmed Catalan central midfield product. Nothing much changed. Villa continued to chase shadows, and also press, mark and nibble at the ankles of shadows whenever they got the chance.

And with 52 minutes gone it was 2-0, the goal scored by Raphinha via a deflected shot, Villa seeming a little hypnotised by a sustained spell of possession and pressure. Pedri made the moment happen with a driving run through the centre followed by a nudged forward pass. It was a fascinating piece of midfield play. Pedri had spent the previous 90 seconds lurking behind João Cancelo, sitting in that space and watching the play, then accelerating on to the ball like a rugby union full-back waiting for the moment to enter the line.

Emery made a triple substitution on the hour mark but before long it was 3-0. This time it was Cancelo’s turn to produce a moment of sublime skill. First he skipped away form McGinn like a man teasing the family labrador. Then he produced an outrageously cinematic low cross with the outside of his right foot for Raphinha to twinkle in and finish neatly again. The goal was his 19th in 13 appearances this season for Barcelona.

Villa came close, clipping the post from a Barcelona head in the crush at a corner. Pedri came off to an adoring ovation: he is just a delight to watch when he has space to direct the flow and tempo of the game. Villa barely got close enough to shake his hand before he was gone.

The final minutes ticked down in front of a steadily emptying stadium. Villa are still well-placed in the first phase mega-table. Emery will crave another chance to get closer to this calibre of opponent before the campaign is out. But this was a demonstration of the gap, still, at the very top.

NASA asks industry for ideas about saving Hubble Space Telescope

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Space.com's Sci-Fi Reader's Club. Read a sci-fi short story every month and join a virtual community of fellow science fiction fans!

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

It's not the first time that NASA has considered sending a robotic spacecraft to help Hubble.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

NASA is reviving discussions to rescue the Hubble Space Telescope, which is operating well but is on track to reenter Earth's atmosphere about six years from now.

The agency issued a request for information (RFI) on Oct. 29 for "in-space servicing" capabilities, which could include boosting Hubble's orbit (which is naturally degrading due to atmospheric drag), or even a mission to "enhance its capabilities" in some way.

Hubble, which launched in April 1990 in partnership with the European Space Agency, was serviced five times by astronauts on space shuttle missions, the most recent being in 2009. The observatory's groundbreaking achievements include helping to show that the expansion of the universe is accelerating — a discovery that won the Nobel Prize — as well as a swath of finds about planets, galaxies, stars and other parts of the universe.

Hubble also regularly teams up with other telescopes for science work, and its observations have led to the publication of at least 23,000 scientific papers, NASA figures suggest. And, despite its age, Hubble remains in high demand. "Astronomers request about seven times as much observing time as is available each year," the agency stated in September.

Hubble is also widely appreciated by the public. People have long enjoyed and celebrated its iconic images, such as the Eagle Nebula's "Pillars of Creation," a gorgeous shot that has been revisited several times over the decades. As such, this is not the first time in recent years that discussions have opened about keeping the famed observatory running for a while longer.

In 2022, for example, billionaire tech entrepreneur Jared Isaacman proposed using SpaceX hardware to mount a Hubble-servicing mission. (Isaacman also purchased and commanded two crewed SpaceX missions to Earth orbit, which flew in September 2021 and September 2024.) NASA declined, however, citing possible risks to the healthy observatory.

But things have changed, apparently. Isaacman, who in December 2025 became NASA administrator, said in an Oct. 30 agency statement that Hubble's science return means the agency should "be looking at every reasonable opportunity to keep it delivering for as long as possible" — especially if doing so would be a cost savings compared with launching another mission.

In an Oct. 30 post on X, Isaacman added that it's "still worth exploring the options" and noted that NASA had previously considered "a far lower-cost solution" to keep the observatory flying. "That was then. Still worth the effort — Hubble deserves it," he added.

The new RFI also allows for alternative scenarios, however, which could include a "safe, controlled reentry into Earth's atmosphere or orbital disposal at the end of Hubble's mission."

Hubble will naturally enter the atmosphere in 2033 or 2034 due to orbital drag, so any attempt to service it would have to be in 2032, the agency stated.

Earlier this year, NASA worked with Katalyst Space Technologies on a $30 million mission that aimed to boost the orbit of the Neil Gehrels Swift Observatory, which is also falling back to Earth due to orbital drag.

That long-shot rescue mission — which was developed quickly to race against accelerated solar activity, which ballooned Earth's atmosphere and increased drag — launched successfully this summer. But control issues prevented Katalyst's LINK spacecraft from meeting up with Swift. Swift is expected to reenter Earth's atmosphere tomorrow (Nov. 4) after 22 years of operations — far past its two-year design lifetime.

Elizabeth Howell (she/her), Ph.D., was a staff writer in the spaceflight channel between 2022 and 2024 specializing in Canadian space news. She was contributing writer for Space.com for 10 years from 2012 to 2024. Elizabeth's reporting includes multiple exclusives with the White House, leading world coverage about a lost-and-found space tomato on the International Space Station, witnessing five human spaceflight launches on two continents, flying parabolic, working inside a spacesuit, and participating in a simulated Mars mission. Her latest book, "Why Am I Taller?" (ECW Press, 2022) is co-written with astronaut Dave Williams.

What My Job Is Like as a Fact Checker at Bon Appétit

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

It takes a village to publish a magazine—and website and app and Youtube channel and more. Phew! Today we’re introducing you to Bon Appétit’s research director Ryan Harrington.

For the non-journalists out there: What does research and fact-checking actually mean?

At Bon Appétit, every story or recipe goes through multiple edits. After that, it’s eligible for research review. At that stage, there are a few major things me and my team looks out for:

Information accuracy: This includes checking the spelling of names and brands, making sure descriptions of restaurant menus and dishes are up to date, reviewing the credibility of the sources we point to, verifying portrayals of quoted individuals, and anything else to make sure that when you read a Bon Appétit story, you know it’s true.

Cultural sensitivity: For a long time, food media has been dominated by a certain privileged perspective. This perpetuated a system that sort of relived the Age of Explorers, with people “discovering” new foods and pigeonholing or exotifying other cultures. A big part of research is honoring different experiences—giving people a platform without making it a spectacle.

Legal concerns: The research department is a bridge between our actual legal team and the editorial team. We keep an eye out for things like libel issues, copyright infringement, food safety risks, protecting sources, or anything else that might need to be discussed with lawyers.

And for the aspiring journalists: How do you become a fact checker? What did you do before joining the Bon Appétit team?

I finished undergrad with a bachelor’s degree in chemistry and then spent six years managing operations of a thoracic surgery laboratory, studying forms of lung cancer. I reached a point in that career where I was asked to go back to school for a master’s or PhD. I realized if I were to invest all of my time in further education, I wanted to study something I felt really strongly about. And that was food media.

I entered the NYU Food Studies master’s program with the goal of studying the cultural impacts of food media. Early on, I got an opportunity to intern at Food & Wine. During my time there, I was asked to help with other projects beyond my internship, and that’s when I started thinking about food media not as just an academic subject but as a viable career. I eventually started working for other publications like Travel & Leisure and Lucky Peach (r.i.p.).

I also tried working at a brewery, though I had started there in February 2020. Dealing with the development of COVID was maybe not the best time to dip my toe in the industry, but I did get a better understanding of the pandemic’s impact.

When I saw the research director position at BA, I knew my previous experiences and education gave me a strong toolset to lead research efforts, and I am grateful for the opportunity. I’ve now been here five years.

You also teach food studies at NYU. How does that inform your work at Bon Appetit?

Whether I am building a lesson plan or reviewing a story for BA, I always try to understand things from the perspective of the recipient: the students or readers. Are we conveying the story clearly? What are they taking away from it? Guiding people to those lightbulb moments, from the classroom to the newsroom is why I love doing what I do.

It’s so cool that you started a research fellowship program here, so students can get real-world experience in media. Tell us more about that.

As mentioned, I got my start in the industry thanks to an internship. I never had formal journalism or media training, but it was thanks to that program that I could build relevant experience and make connections that opened up a chain of other opportunities. When I started at BA, it was my top priority in being able to pay it forward and help mentor a new generation of passionate food lovers.

I teach them the skills to be a great research editor and also try to give each fellow the freedom to pursue their own interests and network while here. Some fellows have spent time in our Test Kitchen, seeing how we develop and edit recipes. Some have pitched and written great stories and published them on our site. Some have helped our photo team and learned what it means to style a recipe for a shoot. Every fellow has different ambitions and I want them to get the most out of their time working with us and further their own goals.

What are your favorite types of stories to work on here at Bon Appétit? What are the most challenging?

I grew up watching Alton Brown’s Good Eats. Forever a scientist at heart, this influence had a life-long impact, nurturing a desire to understand not just the “how” but the “why” behind cooking, while simultaneously emphasizing creativity, ingenuity, and understanding. So when we take a deep, explanatory dive in processes, like our video on how Dr. Arielle Johnson makes a chocolate bar, that’s where I really feel like I am in my element. (Fun fact: Arielle and I were chemistry lab partners during our freshman year of college at NYU.)

The biggest challenge for me is when we cover wine. It has a lot of specific terminology, strict regulations across international borders to keep straight, and foreign spellings with diacritics that I have to be mindful of. Thankfully, I have built a contact list of passionate and educated people on the subject that I can consult when I inevitably have questions.

What’s the funniest rabbit hole you fell down on a BA research assignment?

We ran a story featuring Moe Girton and her bar Gossip Grill back in 2021. During her interview, she discussed the lesbian bar scene in San Diego when she first moved there in the late ’90s. There was a note from the author of the story to me: “note to fact-checkers to verify the names [of these bars].”

Of the three bars she mentioned, two were relatively easy to track down and confirm. But that third one did not have much of a digital footprint. I was looking through local newspaper archives, old community forums and message boards, and anywhere else that people might have left mention of this bar. Though I found some clues, sometimes pieces contradicted each other, and eventually I reached back out to Moe to see if she could give me any more info.

While she couldn’t remember too many specifics, I was able to get the cross streets of where she thought it was located. So I called businesses in that area, one by one, to see if: 1) They were around in the late ’90s and 2) They remembered a neighborhood lesbian bar nearby. A long shot, but as luck would have it, I eventually spoke with the owner of George’s Camera, a photography store that has been in business since 1965. Right next door to him used to be Patti’s Bar, a business he described as a “lesbian and biker friendly bar.” And the best part? He happened to have a photo of their storefront from back then, signs and all.

What’s the Bon Appétit recipe you make most often?

I absolutely love Shilpa Uskokovic’s French Onion Tart. It always impresses guests because it seems fancy and labor-intensive, but in reality, it’s so hands-off that I can be doing any number of other tasks (or taking it easy!) while it comes together. The deliciousness-to-effort ROI is through the roof. And two sheets of [puff pastry](https://www.bonappetit.com/recipes/slideshow/puff-pastry-recipe-ideas) in the box means one for the party, one for breakfast the next morning all to myself.

What’s your favorite obscure food trivia?

I am a product of my time and have spent more than my fair share in debates on “What is a sandwich?” But did you know, in New York, “sandwiches are generally subject to sales tax.” And so, the NY Department of Taxation and Finance has a whole page dedicated to what they consider a sandwich. Spoiler alert: They take a pretty broad stance. Hot dog? Sandwich. Burrito? Sandwich. A freaking buttered bagel? Sandwich.

Other parts of the country hold to a tighter definition. In 2006, the Worcester Superior Court of Massachusetts ruled that a burrito is not a sandwich. When a Qdoba opened in White City Shopping Center, an already present Panera Bread tried to enforce a clause in their lease with the mall that barred any competing sandwich shops from opening. After expert testimony arguing the sides of this case, Judge Jeffrey Locke concluded that a burrito is not a sandwich, allowing Qdoba to operate near Panera without violating the lease.

Maybe there isn’t a perfect answer to this question. Maybe the sandwich was really the friends we made along the way.

Can we convince you to apply to Jeopardy?

The great thing about this job is that I don’t need to know the answer to everything. I just need to know how to find who could answer it. And I don’t think Ken Jennings will let me use Google during filming.

How does AI intersect with or influence your current work?

AI is a powerful tool that can synthesize large volumes of data. But in the world of factual accuracy, there are all sorts of caveats and concerns with the content it generates. It’s still too early to safely adopt blanket applications of AI, but there are ways that it can be useful. Early in my career, when I was the bottom rung of the editorial team, I was often asked to transcribe other reporters’ interview recordings. An hour-long interview could easily take two or three times as long to transcribe by hand with my amateur skills. AI transcription services are far from perfect—and I still need to listen to the recording while reading to verify and fix portions—but it is a huge time saver.

What kitchen tool (or gadget or appliance) are you obsessed with right now?

I love my immersion blender! It can deal with so many tasks in my lovely but small NYC kitchen without needing any valuable countertop real estate. Pasta sauce, soups, salsa, smoothies, it pureés it all and doesn’t even risk any spillage when trying to move things from pot to pitcher and back to pot again.

Special shout out to chef Frank Proto’s tip to use a pair of tongs to juice citrus fruits too. Used this one recently while on vacation in the rental home’s bare-bones kitchen. From marinades to margaritas, we needed a lot of fresh squeezed juice and this trick really made things easier.

The movie or TV show you’d rewatch until the end of time?

One of my favorite shows is Happy Endings, a modestly successful sit-com that ran on ABC from 2011–2013. It’s a Seinfeld template of a group of friends navigating urban young adulthood. And like Seinfeld, it has characters that are acutely aware of the social games that they must struggle through to be a part of a functioning society.

I actually wrote an academic paper discussing how Happy Endings explores food and gender through its use (and sometimes subversion) of the big eater trope. It is performed across two characters of different genders and sexuality and represents a convergent idea of exactly that—performance. Society has expectations that can seem limiting or at least arbitrary. But when we see these characters act in ways that challenge these social conventions, it is encouragement for us to be able to do so as well.

To support our team’s journalism and access our full collection of Test Kitchen–vetted recipes (there are thousands!), please consider subscribing.

Project Hail Mary Author Andy Weir's Sci-Fi Recommendations For Capturing His 'Vibe' [Exclusive]

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

We may receive a commission on purchases made from links.

If you're an aspiring science fiction novelist, you would do well to take the advice of author Andy Weir, who's red hot in publication and Hollywood thanks to the runaway successes of "The Martian" and "Project Hail Mary." That the former was self-published before getting turned into a bestseller and a blockbuster film directed by Ridley Scott — one that starred Matt Damon and earned seven Academy Award nominations, including a nod for Best Picture — is the dream of every up-and-coming writer.

So, genre authors have good reason to hang on Weir's every word, especially when it comes to capturing his profitable vibe. And while I'm a firm believer in finding your own vibe, there's a time-honored tradition in this industry of proving you can write comfortably within established formulae. With Weir, it's about straddling the line between hard science and uplifting, humanistic engagement with a vast universe that needn't be as harsh as we've been led to think. We can always, as Damon's character in "The Martian" says, "science the s*** out of" our various predicaments. I think that's naively hopeful in an age where the United States government is vehemently anti-science (unless it lines the pockets of AI-shilling tech billionaires), but Weir's got that storytelling magic that makes you want to believe we're not too far gone.

Weir is but one man, though, so if you're wondering where you can find his vibe in other work, he shared some suggestions with /Film's Jeremy Mathai, starting with his go-to recommendation:

"Well, if you consider an Andy Weir vibe to be just hard science fiction, with accurate science in there, then you got to go for [James S.A. Corey's] 'The Expanse.' That's really, really good hard sci-fi. That's really solid."

"The Expanse," for those not familiar, deals with a relatively realistic colonization of our solar system that does not initially involve interstellar travel. There's literally a lot of space to fill, but it's subject to the kinds of territorial disputes that have sparked wars on Earth. Ultimately, the narrative expands, bringing in aliens and the like, but, in its television incarnation, Mark Fergus and Hawk Ostby (who brilliantly adapted P.D. James' "The Children of Men" in 2006) approach the scenario with intellectual rigor.

Andy Weir also voiced enthusiasm for Blake Crouch's "Dark Matter," a frantically paced sci-fi novel that's been successfully adapted into an Apple TV series via the author himself. The small screen version of Crouch's parallel universe thriller stars Joel Edgerton and Jennifer Connelly, and it finds the former's character struggling to skip back to his reality (where his family is being appropriated by an alternate universe version of himself).

Finally, Weir is a proponent of Ben Bova's "The Grand Tour" novels, most notably the fourth entry in the book series titled "Mars." As Weir explained:

"That's a fairly hard sci-fi approach to a mission to Mars. It's about the first mission to Mars. It's got some pretty interesting twists in there that turn out to be ... at first, they look a little bit woo-woo, aliens, oh no. Then you find out the twist reveal of what's going wrong, and the thing is absolutely scientific and completely sensible and you're like, 'Ah, nice one.'"

Again, I'd advise you to find your own vibe, but if you want to catch the eye of publishers or studio execs, maybe check these works out and find your way forward the Weir way.

One of the best headphone deals right now is for my favorite headphones: Sony's 1000X series has been around since 2016, with each new model improving on the previous iteration, the latest being the Sony WH-1000XM6, released in 2025. These are the best over-ear headphones I tested in 2025 (or ever, really). Right now, you can get the WH-1000XM6 headphones for $378 (originally $459.99), their lowest price ever, according to price-tracking tools.

The XM6 headphones improve on what were already excellent headphones. Sony upped the count of microphones from eight to 12 when compared to the XM5, which improves more than just how well you sound to someone on a phone call. The microphones are used in almost all of its features, like ANC, Adaptive Sound Control, and the headphones' Ambient Sound mode. They hear your surroundings to adapt their features accordingly. If you switch from a noisy room to a quiet one, the XM6 can switch from ANC to aware mode (if you have Adaptive Sound Control on).

These headphones have a lot more features that you can nerd out on in their companion app. The audio quality is second to none, with the fully customizable EQ giving you plenty of options to get the sound how you like it. The XM6s were not just the best headphones of 2025, but are still the best ones of 2026 and likely to be the best ones for years to come. For $378, you'll be securing the best for a record-low price.

Roma once again threw away a two-goal Champions League advantage in a 3-2 defeat to Manchester United, as after dominating the first half, they repeated past mistakes.

The Giallorossi had a massive problem just hours before kick-off when Pierluigi Gollini hurt his back, so with Mile Svilar, Paulo Dybala, Manu Koné and Lorenzo Pellegrini already out, they had to draft in third-choice goalkeeper Devis Vasquez for his club debut. Rodrigo Mora, Devyne Rensch and Leonardo Balerdi showed this was an attack-minded approach.

Manchester United were in crisis, without a win since September 10 and losing three on the bounce, including a 4-1 Champions League hammering at Como. Lenny Yoro returned from suspension, but Benjamin Sesko, Patrick Dorgu, Marcus Rashford, Manuel Ugarte and Luke Shaw were still out of action.

The deadlock was broken early when Roma’s trademark high press forced an error in midfield, then Rodrigo Mora floated a smart ball over the top to send unmarked Soulé clear to sweep past Sam Lammens at the near post.

Simon Denny Commissioned by New York Magazine to Create Portraits of ‘New AI Elite’ for Power Issue

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

For its latest “Power” issue, New York Magazine commissioned Berlin-based artist Simon Denny to illustrate the article “So Few People, So Much Influence,” about 26 AI engineers, founders, and thinkers whose “influence on the AI landscape far outstrips their fame.”

These include people like Logan Graham of Anthropic, whose job is to try and push AI models to do terrifying things like build a bioweapon or enact a cyberattack; Beth Barnes, the founder of METR, a nonprofit that tests AI models before they are released; and Brandan Foddy of Mercor, which trains AI models to take over more jobs and industries. Related Articles Italian Police Used AI to Identify $200 M. Worth of Stolen Art and Antiquities Jewish Digital Cultural Recovery Project Adds AI to Provenance Search Tool

The article, a collaboration with Transformer, a Substack newsletter about the power and politics of AI, includes a footnote explaining that Denny used AI to generate portraits that had the look and feel of VHS tapes. Indeed, each of Denny’s Technologists Portraits, as the series is called, shows a smiling figure with a fake-seeming backdrop depicted in the kind of crackly, oversaturated palette associated with “vintage” media like VHS. The dated, distorted style of the portraits is a powerful contrast to the the power and influence held by this cohort of virtually unknown AI experts.

“With each major paradigm shift in the past 20 years,” Denny explained, “I have tried to map and unpack some of this community’s stories as they narrate our experience of a changing technological world. The distortions of the final compositions are traces of the changes that are coming and the people bringing them about.”

Denny has long explored the intersection between art and technology, and has lately been writing about the topic as well. (In April, he wrote about the aesthetics of defense tech and in October about Polymarket, both for Artforum.) He represented New Zealand in the 56th Venice Biennale in 2015, where his show, “Secret Power,” turned internet servers into vitrines to display leaked documents and visual content about the National Security Agency and an intelligence alliance between the US, UK, Canada, Australia, and New Zealand known as “Five Eyes.”

More recently, Denny’s piece Output 0971 (2025), from his “Machine Painting” series, was included in the exhibition “Sentient Earth” at Amant. “I’m interested in the stories that are woven by the people making the technology that we end up using,” Denny told ARTnews. “It’s hard to parse what’s mythology and what’s truth when we’re confronting machine learning; it’s really unfamiliar even to those actors who are building it. This is why myth making is so important in the tech space. How does all of this become familiar and normalized?”
