Ted Schlein’s Ballistic Ventures seeks $490M for new funds amid AI-driven cybersecurity boom

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Ballistic Ventures, the cybersecurity-focused venture capital firm co-founded by former Kleiner Perkins partner Ted Schlein (pictured above, second from the right), is seeking to raise $490 million across two new funds, TechCrunch has learned through regulatory filings.

The San Francisco-based VC firm filed with the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission this week to raise $360 million for its third flagship fund, matching the size of its previous fund raised in 2024, and another $130 million for a separate vehicle called Ballistic Ventures Fund B. Neither fund had recorded any sales at the time of filing.

The fundraising plans come as the growing use of artificial intelligence has raised concerns about more sophisticated cyberattacks. The rise of AI agents has also created new opportunities for cybersecurity startups and the venture capital firms backing them.

Founded in late 2021, Ballistic Ventures’ founding team includes Schlein, former AT&T Cybersecurity president Barmak Meftah, and Roger Thornton, who co-founded security companies Fortify Software and AlienVault. The firm has backed companies, including Talon Cyber Security, acquired by Palo Alto Networks, and Pangea, bought by CrowdStrike, alongside startups such as Armadin, GetReal, Nudge Security, and Oligo Security.

Ballistic’s ties to storied venture capital firm Kleiner Perkins, where Schlein spent more than two decades, help set it apart from rivals, a cybersecurity investor at another VC firm told TechCrunch. Its investment strategy, however, is similar to those of other cybersecurity-focused investors, including Forgepoint Capital and Ten Eleven Ventures, the person said.

In 2022, Ballistic announced its $300 million debut fund and raised another $360 million for its second flagship fund two years later. It also raised about $130 million for a separate vehicle called Ballistic Ventures Fund A, per PitchBook data. The new Fund B is targeting a similar amount.

U.S. cybersecurity startups raised $11.5 billion in 2025, the highest since 2022, a recent report by JPMorgan Chase said, citing PitchBook data. AI-enabled companies accounted for about 72% of U.S. cybersecurity venture deals through May this year, compared with 36% in 2019, the report said.

Separately, Microsoft, in its annual Digital Defense Report released last month, warned that hackers are increasingly using AI for activities ranging from phishing and social engineering to malware development. The technology is allowing attackers to operate faster and at greater scale, even as many of their underlying techniques remain unchanged.

Ballistic Ventures declined to comment on its fundraising plans.

Guadalupe Rosales’s Portable Archive of East LA

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

In two new books, the artist weaves her archive of photos, ephemera, and flyers into the story of ’90s Chicanx youth culture, tracing both a personal and collective coming-of-age.

If someone had described Los Angeles youth culture in the late 1990s, they might have invoked towheaded surfers, Sunset Strip hair bands, Beverly Hills teens, and the graffiti-soaked clubs that harbored New Wave and punk. Thanks to Guadalupe Rosales, our view of that time is now much more expansive.

Since 2015, the Los Angeles artist has collected the stories of the city’s Chicanx and Latinx youth — in particular, women and girls — via a pair of wildly popular Instagram pages, @veteranas_and_rucas and @map_pointz, which have become a collective archive of pre-internet adolescent life in the ’80s and ’90s. Big hair. Lined lips. Body-hugging tanks and baggy jeans. Not to mention the party flyers bearing titles like “Bad Influence” and “Titty Juice,” with their irreverent promises of liberation.

Over the years, Rosales has displayed this ephemera in imaginative ways, often in architectonic environments that pulse with the energy of nocturnal gatherings. In her installation for In Minor Keys, the international exhibition at the 61st Venice Biennale, infinity mirrors edged with neon are embedded with artifacts and suspended on a steel structure evoking the form of a SoCal bungalow.

A new project, however, finds the artist presenting her archive in more intimate ways. East of the River: A Memoir of Los Angeles Girlhood, published this fall, assembles many of the photos, flyers, news clips, and testimonials that followers have sent her for more than a decade. To that, she adds her own photographs and experiences, relaying the poignant story of her Eastside upbringing and the ways in which revelry with friends offered a respite from troubles both at home and in the larger city: the 1992 uprising, growing anti-immigrant sentiment, and gang violence. The latter claimed the life of her beloved cousin, Ever Sánchez, who was killed at a party in 1996. “It took me years to forgive those who took his life,” she writes, “but eventually I was able to turn the grief into altars and memorials and storytelling.”

"Steam's review queue is clogged up by AI slop": Salacious Fire Emblem-ish Food Devils gets a delay because its devs think Valve is inundated with genAI games

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

You might have seen that for today's grub, Edwin decided to in part feed The Maw with Food Devils, an incredibly salacious, foodie take on Fire Emblem, as it was meant to release today. The only problem is, it's not actually out yet. That might prompt you to call Edwin a rotten liar and march up to his castle at the top of the hill on a dark and stormy night with pitchforks and torches, but I beseech you to wait, because it's actually Steam's fault. And potentially, it's the fault of all those awful AI games that have been flooding Steam recently.

In a Steam blog post shared earlier today, developer Studio Daimon explained why the game isn't able to be released today, first offering a bit of context: if you want to put your game on Steam, you have to submit for approval so that it matches what you've described and doesn't harbour malware, amongst other reasons. As the post explains, "before submitting a build, devs see a disclaimer that says 'This review typically takes 3-5 business days, but please allow up to 7 business days in case feedback needs to be addressed'." Except the team submitted their build on October 12th, more than 20 days ago, with no approval in sight.

The reason? "We can only assume it's because Steam's review queue is clogged up by AI slop, but the whole process is now taking several weeks for some developers." For some context, Game Discovery Tool did some statistics magic and found that more than 50% of games released in October on Steam featured an AI disclosure. Valve have required that games disclose AI use since January 2024, and a year on from then in January 2025 only just over 14% of games featured such a disclosure. In January 2026, that jumped up to a little over 25%, and now here we are at more than 50%.

And to be clear, Steam releases have gone up too. According to SteamDB data, a little over 2000 games were released on the platform in October 2025. But in October 2026, that has jumped up to almost 4000. Correlation is obviously not causation, so take that with a pinch of salt. Still, it paints a concerning image over AI usage, and potentially sounds like it's affecting how Valve operates. Almost as if allowing free reign over AI is bad, or something!

Unfortunately for Studio Daimon, there's no estimate on when Food Devils might launch. "Even now, we don't know when Steam will give us the final OK to finally release our game," they wrote. "We sent another ticket last week saying that the situation was urgent and that we might miss our release date because of this, but it also got ignored. I'm sure that their staff is just too overwhelmed at the moment, but these sudden and massive delays have affected many developers throughout October, with some even being forced to postpone their releases... including us."

They're hoping to still be able to release it by the end of the week, but "can't make any plans or promises because it's out of our control." Perhaps in the meantime, for those who like what's cooking in the devs' kitchen, we could throw the game a wishlist to be ready for whenever it does launch.

There's a New Luxury Sleeper Train Route Coming to the French Riviera Next Spring

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

As someone who travels a lot for work, I would give anything to swap a flight for a train—even if that means spending eight hours on the rails.

Thankfully, it's easy to travel Europe by train at nearly any price point. And although I am a big fan of modern high-speed routes, the most romantic way to see the continent is certainly aboard one of Belmond's gorgeous luxury trains, which travel to destinations such as the British countryside, the Amalfi Coast, and the Scottish Highlands.

But in 2027, my bucket-list Belmond journey is a glamorous new addition to its already impressive collection of itineraries: the French Riviera.

The Venice Simplon-Orient-Express offers three cabin types: Historic Cabins, Suites, and Grand Suites.

On May 10, 2027, the Venice Simplon-Orient-Express is launching a new route from Paris to Monaco, traveling to Monte Carlo for the first time in its history.

The journey, which will become a regular addition to the train's annual schedule, will take passengers through some of the most spectacular scenery along the French Riviera before arriving in the glamorous principality.

The new route will take travelers to Monte Carlo for the first time in the train's history.

The experience begins in Paris, where passengers will board the train's distinctive midnight blue carriages before settling into their accommodation for the night. There are several options to choose from, including the classic Historic Cabins, Suites, and Grand Suites, each drawing inspiration from the train's Art Deco heritage.

For those looking to really splash out, there's also L'Observatoire, an exclusive carriage created in collaboration with French artist JR. Designed as a private sanctuary on the rails, it comes complete with a bedroom, an en-suite bathroom featuring a freestanding bathtub, a spacious living area, a library, and even a secret tearoom.

As evening approaches, guests can enjoy dinner in one of the train's two restaurant cars, Étoile du Nord and L'Oriental, before heading to the famous Bar Car 3674 for cocktails and live piano music.

But perhaps the most exciting part of the new journey comes the following morning. After waking up to breakfast served in their cabins, passengers will be treated to sweeping views of the Mediterranean as the train makes its way along the Côte d'Azur. The route passes the red cliffs of the Massif de l'Estérel and travels through Saint-Raphaël towards Cannes and Nice. The train will then continue towards Monaco before arriving at Monaco-Monte-Carlo station.

Breakfast served aboard the Venice-Simplon Orient Express.

Monte Carlo also has a surprising connection to the train's history. In 1977, two original train carriages were purchased at an auction in the principality, helping set in motion the project that would eventually become the luxury train we know today.

“Introducing the destination feels like a natural continuation for the Venice-Simplon Orient Express, bringing the romance of luxury rail travel to a locale that has always understood the art of arrival,” says Pascal Deyrolle, Belmond's Europe area managing director for trains. “There's also something rather special about coming back to Monte Carlo, given the role it played in the train's history.”

The new journey is part of Belmond's expanding collection of European rail experiences, alongside the second year of its “Villeggiatura by Train” itinerary, which connects the Venice Simplon-Orient-Express with Belmond hotels in Italy.

Of course, a journey this luxurious comes with a price tag to match. Tickets for the new Paris-to-Monte-Carlo route start from €4,870 (approximately $5,450) per person, based on two people sharing a Historic Cabin. The fare includes the overnight journey, all meals with sommelier-selected wines, unlimited soft drinks, transfers, and 24-hour steward service.

Worker risks his life using soda bottles to extinguish raging electrical fire

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

A utility worker in Venezuela has gone viral after using bottles of soda to put out a blazing electrical transformer in a dangerous maneuver that could have ended in disaster.

The incident occurred on November 2 in Valle de la Pascua, Venezuela, where a worker reportedly employed by the state-owned electricity company Corpoelec found himself facing a transformer engulfed in flames.

Rather than reaching for a fire extinguisher, however, the man decided to use something considerably less conventional: two-liter bottles of soda, identified as the Venezuelan brand ‘Glup’.

The employee was elevated in the bucket of a maintenance truck when he began shaking the bottles and launching them toward the burning equipment.

Footage of the incident shows the worker throwing the bottles directly at the transformer before immediately ducking for cover as flames erupt toward his position.

Valle de la Pascua Estado Guárico 🚨 Apaga el transformador con refresco, sucedió en Valle de la Pascua… pic.twitter.com/kAzD0GOn5q

He repeated the process three times, with the fire dying down after his efforts.

But how did it actually work? Carbonated drinks contain carbon dioxide (CO2), which can help suppress flames by displacing oxygen, while the liquid itself may have helped cool the burning equipment.

The extraordinary footage quickly spread across social media, with viewers stunned by the worker’s unconventional firefighting technique.

However, experts have warned that the stunt was incredibly dangerous and could have cost the worker his life.

Speaking to Venezuelan outlet El Pitazo, electrical engineer Jesús Olivares explained that the carbon dioxide in fizzy drinks does not make them a safe substitute for specialized fire extinguishers.

He warned that the liquid could conduct electricity, potentially causing a fatal shock, while the sudden bursts of flames also put the worker at serious risk of burns.

Man pulls gun on streamer pouring fake gasoline on cars for prank

Man thwarts shoplifting by throwing a Coke bottle at thief

Olivares also questioned the apparent lack of protective equipment, noting that the employee appeared to be working without essential safety gear.

Although the flames appeared to subside, the cause of the fire remains unknown, and there has been no confirmation of whether anyone was injured.

Microsoft improves Windows 11 performance on 8 GB PCs as RAM prices keep climbing

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

We've got a hunch the memory crunch is taking a bite out of Windows revenue

With RAM getting more expensive, Microsoft is putting more effort into making Windows 11 run better on systems with just 8 GB of memory.

Microsoft said on Tuesday that it was "raising the bar for 8 GB Windows 11 PCs," following a renewed focus on memory optimization earlier this year. The changes come as Redmond continues pushing more expensive Copilot+ PCs, which require at least 16 GB of RAM, amid a memory crunch fueled by AI infrastructure demand.

Now, Microsoft says a low-end machine with its 8 GB of precious RAM will be up to 20 percent more responsive and 40 percent faster at multitasking thanks to improvements made in memory management.

The tweaks are relatively simple, as Microsoft explained, and seem more about adjusting back to the reality that its flagship operating system needs to function on the hardware people can actually afford to purchase if and when they’re forced to upgrade.

Windows 11 will now keep up to 2 GB more active data in memory before shunting it to slower storage, as one example. In another instance, Windows will connect directly to Edge when the browser is already running in the background, allowing it to launch up to 33 percent faster, according to Microsoft. Microsoft apps are also being made more efficient, with Teams able to enable Efficiency Mode by default on 8 GB machines, which Microsoft said will make it launch "up to 20 percent faster."

Memory efficiency is also being improved by tweaking things like Widgets, which will only load when used instead of always being on, and decreasing the time when Edge tabs go to sleep from two hours to 15 minutes by default. Microsoft said it’s enabling third-party apps to make use of those tweaks, too.

Microsoft also included benchmark data in the blog post from a recent bit of research it commissioned. The benchmark report compares a range of 2026 Windows machines with models from 2021, finding that a 2026 Dell XPS 13 with 8 GB of RAM performed about as well as a 2021 Dell XPS 13 with 16 GB across selected workloads.

What that means, says Microsoft, is that “the complete PC, not memory capacity alone, shapes the experience.”

In other words, please buy some new Windows hardware, even if it’s expensive - we’re begging you. Software optimization can only do so much.

“For many people, 8 GB is a great fit for the things they do every day,” Microsoft EVP of Windows and devices Pavan Davuluri wrote in the blog post. “Demanding workloads, such as 3D modeling, advanced video editing, or large-scale data processing benefit from 16 GB of memory or more.”

It’s no surprise Microsoft is improving the performance of lower end systems at the same time it’s subtly asking users to buy newer kit, even if it’s just an 8 GB machine - Microsoft may have done well in the past two quarters, but it’s no thanks to Windows devices.

Microsoft’s Q4 2026 earnings in late July saw the company rake in cash more broadly, while simultaneously reporting a 7 percent decrease in Windows OEM and devices revenue.

Likewise, in its fiscal Q1 2027 results, reported just last week, Microsoft again brought home a mountain of net income, with adjusted earnings reaching $36.4 billion, up 18 percent from the same period last year. Windows OEM and devices revenue, however, was down a whopping 22 percent. It’s hard to think of a reason beyond the fact that hardware prices have shot up with more force than a SpaceX rocket - memory prices are likely to actually make it to Mars, unlike Starship - leaving both consumers and enterprises to stretch those upgrade cycles.

If you need to get the most out of that 8 GB Windows 11 PC, you’ll find the latest improvements in 26H2, build 26300.9818 or later. Microsoft is also encouraging users to keep its branded apps up to date, as “some improvements arrive through app updates or through Microsoft Store.”  ®

Sun Joe Electric Wood Chipper Review: Turn That Storm Cleanup Into Usable Mulch

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

This light-duty wood chipper is ideally suited and priced for the average yard.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. Why Trust Us?

My backyard runs up to a wooded area. Although there are few trees on my actual property, any storm that comes through pushes twigs and branches from the wooded area onto my lawn. And with several large rose bushes to tend to, I have a fair amount of clippings to break down. Gathering this debris up in a large waste container has been my approach for cleaning up my yard. But leaving this debris on the curb to be hauled off to the local landfill or burning it in my fire pit seemed like a waste, especially since I have landscaping beds and a garden that could benefit from this yard waste.

Purchasing a gas-powered wood chipper to break this waste down into usable mulch felt like overkill. Plus, I didn’t have space to store a gas chipper, nor did I want to fork over $600 or $700 for one of these machines. Enter the Sun Joe CJ603E electric wood chipper.

It doesn’t have the wood-chipping power of a gas wood chipper like the Champion portable gas wood chipper or the Earthquake K32 wood chipper, but it does what I need it to do. It can chip small branches and twigs, and costs a fraction of a gas-powered wood chipper. Plus, it’s a manageable weight and size, so it’s easy to move around and store. I spent several months testing the Sun Joe for our Best Wood Chippers buying guide earlier this year and have been using it to assist with yard cleanup ever since. It’s proven to be up to the task.

What better way to test the Sun Joe CJ603E’s wood-chipping abilities than to feed it as many twigs and branches as possible? And since my property backs up to a densely wooded area, I had plenty of fodder for it to chew on. Keeping to its listed max branch diameter of 1.73 inches, I crammed dead, dry branches into the Sun Joe.

Along with its ability to chew up dead wood, I also wanted to see how it handled green material, so I used the CJ603E to break down clippings trimmed from several large rose bushes on my property. Lastly, I also considered how well the Sun Joe was able to chip material into bits that were small enough to serve as mulch.

The Sun Joe CJ603E is one of the easiest wood chippers I’ve tested. For starters, assembly took just a few minutes. It only weighs 38 pounds and sits on two wheels, so moving it from my garage to my backyard felt effortless compared to moving gas wood chippers that weigh well over 100 pounds. The Sun Joe CJ603E uses a standard wall outlet for power, so it requires an extension cord, which limits its range compared to a gas-powered model. That said, the CJ603E also starts at the push of a button, so there’s no wrestling with a pull starter. At about 3 feet tall assembled on its stand, the Sun Joe is roughly the size of a counter-height bar stool with a backrest. That’s much smaller than a full-size gas wood chipper, which is important for garages like mine that are strapped for space.

We at Popular Mechanics are already familiar with Sun Joe’s line of wood chippers, having tested an earlier model a few years ago. Sun Joe’s CJ603E is a clear upgrade. The addition of a pressure plate that forces branches and twigs up against the blades gives this updated model more cutting power than its predecessor. The results were noticeable during testing.

I began my testing with dried-out dead wood, beginning with smaller twigs before working up to larger branches. The Sun Joe handled it all well, even consuming branches that were pushing beyond its listed 1.73-inch-diameter capacity.

The CJ603E is a prolific eater. I was able to feed sticks into it continuously without having to wait for the blades to chew through already-loaded material. And since the machine pulls sticks into it, there’s no need to use a separate stick to push material down its throat (though the CJ603E does include one).

The CJ603E does have an Achilles’ heel, and that’s with thicker, greener branches that exceed 1.5 inches in diameter. This “wet” wood had a tendency to clog the output chute, creating torn pieces rather than cleanly cut chips. While this wasn’t a huge issue for me, it could be for someone who uses a wood chipper to clean up live branches brought down during a storm.

Another benefit of the CJ603E is that it operates more quietly than the loud gas-powered models I’ve tested and does not produce exhaust, though you should still wear hearing protection when operating it. The CJ603E also requires far less maintenance since it doesn’t use a gas engine.

The Sun Joe CJ603E produces wood chips suitable for mulching, but don’t expect them to have a uniform look. Depending on what I was chipping, the CJ603E produced anywhere from 1/2-inch- to 1-inch-long wood chips, which was coarser than I expected given its listed 21:1 reduction ratio. I also noticed that the CJ603E would allow some longer twigs to pass through without being completely chopped. Since these irregularities make the wood chips less ideal for use in decorative landscaping, I use them as a base layer with a top layer of commercial mulch in my landscaping.

Sun Joe’s CJ603E wood chipper does what it’s designed for well. It can chip branches and twigs up to 1.73 inches in diameter into wood chips for easy disposal or use as mulch. Its compact size and comparatively light weight make it easy to move from garage or shed to yard and back again, and it doesn’t take up a huge amount of space in a shed or garage.

Homeowners will also appreciate the ease of use an electric wood chipper offers compared to gas models, since there’s no gas-powered engine to start or maintain. These factors, along with its low purchase cost, make it a smart option for homeowners looking for something that can handle modest yard cleanup.

That said, it’s important to understand that the Sun Joe CJ603E does not match the power of gas-powered wood chippers designed for residential use. Gas-powered wood chippers are more capable, with most able to chew through branches up to 3 inches in diameter. Their powerful engines also make them capable of moving through branches at a higher rate. There’s also a difference in durability. While the Sun Joe has numerous plastic parts, gas wood chippers have mainly metal construction, so they can better endure bigger jobs and more frequent use.

The Sun Joe is also a dedicated wood chipper. Unlike chipper/shredders, the CJ603E doesn’t have a shredder function that will reduce leaves into mulch. If you're looking for a wood chipper that can handle bigger branches and shred leaves, we suggest one of our other recommended models below.

This gas wood chipper chewed through 3-inch branches with ease when we tested it, and it also doubles as a very effective leaf shredder. Couple that performance with a reasonable price tag, and it’s an ideal gas wood chipper for homeowners with wooded lots to manage.

This is the most affordable gas wood chipper we could find that still has the chops to get the job done. It’s capable of grinding through branches up to 3 inches in diameter and has a chute that makes it easy to deposit those wood chips directly into your landscaping. It only chips wood, though. If you’re looking for something that can also shred leaves, you’ll need to look elsewhere.

Earthquake’s K32 wood chipper boasted some of the most impressive wood-chipping power we experienced of any of the models we tested. What’s more, it produces some of the smallest wood chips. If you’re looking for a machine to create high-quality mulch for your landscaping, then spring for this one.

Tony Carrick is a full-time freelance writer who specializes in technology, home improvement, DIY, home security, and outdoor recreation. He’s tested and written about everything from home security systems to power tools to gas grills. His product guides, how-to articles, and feature stories can be found in such publications as Bob Vila, Angi, U.S. News and World Report, Field & Stream, Futurism, and Switchful. When Tony isn’t writing, he can be found working on his latest home improvement effort at his home in North Carolina.

Colin Aylesworth attended Binghamton University and is a Deputy Editor with the Gear team at Popular Mechanics and Runner’s World, where he has been since 2022. Prior to joining Hearst Magazines, he worked for 11 years at U.S. News and World Report on the autos team.

His personal pursuits fit in well with the work he does for PM and RW. Aylesworth was on his college’s club rowing team and has maintained a routine running habit through most of his adult life as a means of getting exercise and spending time outdoors. Running creates a hearty appetite, which brings us to Popular Mechanics. That publication's expertise in the area of grilling is relatable to Aylesworth, who is an adherent to charcoal grilling. In fact, he’s been primarily using the same Weber charcoal kettle he bought on Craigslist in 2012.

Aylesworth also has experience in the lawn mower industry. Not only has he been mowing his and neighbor’s lawns during adolescence, one of his more recent endeavors is a side gig of repairing and restoring gas lawn mowers given up by their previous owners. Since joining PM, he has gotten more involved with the publication’s mower testing efforts and especially enjoys testing out new electric and zero turn mowers.

as humanity dreams of mars, artist mari nagem asks us to reconsider life on earth

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

the artist questions whether humanity would carry its extractive relationship with nature into the worlds it hopes to colonize.

At New York’s CUE Art, Brazilian artist Mari Nagem draws a connection between humanity’s dreams of colonizing Mars and the ecosystems we are struggling to sustain on Earth. Her work explores a curious contradiction in our relationship with technology: we are developing increasingly sophisticated ways to survive on distant planets while overlooking the extraordinary systems of life that already exist around us.

In her show, The Tropics, The Moon, and Mars, Nagem imagines what such a future might look like. Saturated red light fills the gallery, illuminating sculptural motorized branches and small ceramic formations. The installation recalls an unfamiliar planetary landscape, but its materials and references actually come from Earth’s changing environments. Slightly unsettling, the effect brings the promise of extraterrestrial exploration into conversation with the ecological crises happening here on Earth.

Much of Mari Nagem’s practice begins with scientific research, which she interprets through sculpture and digital media. In Flora Futura (The Survival Dance), bare branches mounted on motorized stands move slowly against a red-lit backdrop. The work draws on ESECAFLOR, a long-running experiment in the Brazilian Amazon where researchers divert rainfall from sections of forest to study how trees respond to prolonged drought.

The artist takes this question of survival into an imagined future. The branches sway and cast shifting shadows, their movements suggesting both growth and decline. It’s difficult to tell whether these strange mechanical plants are adapting to their environment or slowly dying. The uncertainty reflects the experiment’s larger concern: how might forests continue to survive as their climate changes?

The relationship between technological experimentation and environmental change also appears in 41°C (2025), a 43-meter-long ground painting created for the Emílio Goeldi Zoobotanical Park in Belém during COP30. Its vivid colors trace the Tefé River, where water temperatures reached 41°C during the devastating Amazonian drought of 2023, contributing to the deaths of hundreds of river dolphins.

Drawing on satellite imagery and thermal data, Nagem transforms an environmental event into something visitors can encounter at the scale of their own bodies.

Both works make processes that are difficult to observe feel unusually immediate. One animates a forest under stress, while the other gives physical form to an extreme temperature. Through these translations, Nagem considers how scientific tools might change the way we perceive the living world.

This question becomes especially interesting in The Right to Shade (2026), which examines access to shade in Fortaleza, Brazil. Using satellite imagery and AI-assisted analysis, Nagem and her collaborators map the distribution of trees and buildings across the city, revealing how exposure to intense sunlight varies between neighborhoods.

The project began with an interest in architectural solutions for cooling urban environments, including a new version of the traditional Brazilian cobogó, a perforated brick that filters sunlight. Yet the research eventually turned toward something far older and more complex: trees. Their canopies provide cooling through an intricate biological system, raising questions about why cities continue to develop artificial solutions while neglecting the living infrastructure already available.

Nagem uses advanced computational tools to draw attention to forms of intelligence that developed long before human technology. It suggests a different way of imagining the future, one in which architectural and technological progress begins by understanding our dependence on other living organisms.

Elsewhere, Nagem considers what happens when those organisms disappear altogether. In Siren (2024), created with Leandro Lima for SFER IK Museion in Mexico, artificial intelligence is used to imagine the songs of extinct South American birds. The sounds emerge from sculptural speakers resembling nests, combining digitally fabricated ceramics with traditional handcraft.

The installation raises an uneasy possibility. Technology might allow us to imagine the voices of vanished species, although the actual birds and their ecological relationships remain lost. The work sits between scientific speculation and a kind of synthetic remembrance, questioning what it means to recreate traces of nature through machines.

Across her practice, Nagem returns to the distance between what humans can invent and what we have learned to understand. From artificial birdsong to the complex intelligence of a tree canopy, her work encourages us to reconsider the place of technology within the natural world.

As visions of future settlements on the Moon and Mars grow increasingly detailed, she brings our attention back to the species and environments with which we already share a planet.

project: The Tropics, The Moon, and Mars, alongside selected works from 2024–2026

exhibition dates: July 11th — October 10th, 2026

<i><em>Reggie Dinkins</em></i> Is Still the Funniest Show on TV

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Tracy Morgan and Daniel Radcliffe return for season 2 of their hilarious NBC sitcom somehow even wittier than before.

It’s a gross exaggeration to say that television is a lost art, but we can maybe agree that it has changed. Big-time. But sadly, not all of it is for the better. The binge model pigeonholes every show into a novel, scriptwriters tragically factor in second-screen doomscrolling, and the half-hour situational comedy has been driven to near extinction. Which is why I thank the television gods for The Fall and Rise of Reggie Dinkins and then thank them again that it’s back so soon.

Still sharp, still absurd, and still just plain funny, The Fall and Rise of Reggie Dinkins proves sophomores aren’t always in a slump, with a new season that picks up from season 1’s high point. “More of the same” isn’t bad news here, and if the series can keep pace, we might have a proper TV MVP when other sitcoms can’t make the cut.

Season 2 of Reggie Dinkins, which premiered November 2 on NBC, catches up with Daniel Radcliffe’s anguished auteur, Arthur Tobin, as he documents the show’s hero, Reggie Dinkins (Tracy Morgan). Dinkins is a disgraced football superstar, and he’s hell-bent on rehabilitating his public profile. Arthur still lives in the Dinkins’ crowded New Jersey mansion that also houses Reggie’s levelheaded ex-wife and manager Monica (Erika Alexander), their son Carmelo (Jalyn Hall), Reggie’s younger fiancée Brina (Precious Way), and Bobby Moynihan as Rusty, Reggie’s freeloading BFF who is too integral to the family’s cohesion to kick to the curb.

This season, Arthur grows ever closer to the Dinkins clan, despite his own hyperawareness of his documentary ethics violations. (Episode 2 of this season at last interrogates how compromised the in-progress project has been since the start.) Most of the ensemble, Radcliffe and Morgan included, exists in a sort of stasis—this is a network sitcom, after all—but it’s Precious Way who shines as the much-improved standout of the bunch.

Formerly “just” the younger woman in Reggie’s life last season, Brina has grown into the soul of the show. Along with Way’s buoyant charisma, there’s a newfound depth to Brina that wasn’t present in her before. I’ll admit, I thought she was grating at first blush, and I was ready to write her off as a shallow antagonist who didn’t seem involved with Reggie or the family in earnest. But in season 2, she’s evolved into a phenomenal supporting player who instantly brightens the room and contrasts beautifully with the others, most of all Radcliffe’s flummoxed British artiste. (A throwaway gag involving her “Voice Bucket Challenge” had me cracking up at my desk.) It’s a mere single component upgrade we’re talking about here, but it elevates Reggie Dinkins to a much higher degree this time around.

Jalyn Hall, too, grows in his once-thankless role as Reggie and Monica’s teenage son, Carmelo. Through a new overarching storyline involving a forbidden romance, which becomes a linchpin for most of season 2’s first half, Carmelo crystallizes in his specific variant of “Blerd” that is more refreshing than derivative. He’s not just “The Geek,” as you’d find in most sitcoms. He’s fluid enough to perform the function without killing the buzz.

The first four episodes of the season, provided to the press, keep up season 1’s winning formula—22-minute-sitcom hijinks that unfold against an airy atmosphere with well-timed punchlines. Daniel Radcliffe and Tracy Morgan further assert their powers as TV’s unlikeliest duo, and I can’t get enough of them. I pray there are yet more improbable excuses to ensure multiple seasons of a single documentary’s production. Sure, the rhythms are familiar now, but it’s welcome fun. Reggie Dinkins is a potent reminder of what television used to be and what it still can be if we’re brave enough to let it.

Nextorage Debuts New 1TB SD Card for Pro Video Work

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Nextorage Debuts New 1TB SD Card for Pro Video Work

Nextorage Corporation has announced the NX-F2AE Series, a trio of new UHS-II SDXC memory cards that promise high-end speed and reliability for demanding video workflows.

While a lot of attention in recent years has been on CFexpress Type A and Type B cards, many photographers and videographers still use SD cards in their cameras, either as their primary storage or as backup in case they fill up their CFexpress card. SD cards are an important tool for many creators.

Nextorage says the NX-F2AE Series is its Advanced Edition SDXC UHS-II card, which means the cards were designed specifically for professional video production applications.

“As high-resolution, high-bitrate video formats such as 4K and 8K continue to become increasingly common, the demand for memory cards capable of delivering both large storage capacities and stable long-duration recording has grown significantly,” Nextorage explains. “The NX-F2AE Series was designed to meet these requirements, providing reliable and uninterrupted recording performance for demanding video applications.”

As expected, given the billing, the new SD cards have a V90 rating, the highest video speed class for SD cards. This means the cards guarantee a minimum sustained write speed of 90 MB/s. Overall, the cards promise read and write speeds up to 300 MB/s.

Nextorage recommends using the cards alongside its NX-DF1CL Dual-Slot SD Card Reader. The card reader supports the UHS-II interface for both slots, meaning that each card can offload files at full speed.

“Whether shooting in 4K, 6K, or 8K resolution, or capturing high-frame-rate footage, the card delivers stable performance under heavy data workloads while minimizing the risk of dropped frames and recording interruptions,” Nextorage promises.

The new cards are available in 256GB, 512GB, and 1TB capacities. For video applications in particular, larger cards are often preferred as 8K footage can take up considerable space in short order.

Professionals have rigorous reliability demands as well, and Nextorage says its new SD cards have passed a series of seven durability and protection tests. Specifically, the cards are rated to operate in temperatures from -25° to 85° C (-13° to 185° F), shock-resistant for drops from heights up to 1.5 meters (4.9 feet), and bend-resistant up to 10 Newtons. The cards are also tested for 10,000 connector matings and are resistant to X-rays, magnets, and electrostatic discharge.

The Nextorage NX-F2AE Series SD cards start at $299.99 for the 256GB size. The 512GB card is $529.99, while the 1TB one is $999.99. All three cards are available today.

I Went To Bermuda &amp; Fell In Love With The Dark ‘N’ Stormy

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

You know an island is really fun when it has a national cocktail. Before heading to Bermuda, I didn’t even realize its national drink was the Dark ‘n’ Stormy. I should have, given its deep roots and ties to rum, especially Gosling Rum. And even though I knew what the drink was, I had never had one. I was excited to try my first one right where it was created!

Long story short: I was not disappointed. The Dark ‘n’ Stormy isn’t too sweet, has a strong ginger kick, and, best of all, is easy to make at home. I also love a layered drink. Even one that looks like an eerie cloud is so pretty to look at. I think this drink finally turned me into a rum lover.

When I normally think of a rum drink, I think of all the fun, tropical ones, like a piña colada or strawberry daiquiri. Perfect for sipping on the beach or by the pool.

But the Dark ‘n’ Stormy is made for the fall and winter. It’s spicy from the ginger beer, which pairs so well with the sweet molasses flavor of the dark rum. Get the spiciest ginger beer you can get your hands on. The more ginger flavor, the better, I think. A chilly fall night deserves this drink. If there's a thunderstorm, major bonus points.

Bermudians are natural storytellers, and after hearing any of their stories, you’d be hard-pressed not to try their favorite drink. Legend has it that after being served this drink, a sailor thought it looked like the worst kind of cloud, one only a fool or a dead man would sail under. It makes you shudder to order one, but it’s too good to stop you.

You need two ingredients to make a Dark ‘n’ Stormy. Gosling Rum and ginger beer. It is common to add a squeeze of lime, which is always a good idea. I don't find it totally necessary because I like keeping it on the warm, darker side. The simple, straightforward version is incredible as is. If you have a lime on hand, then go for it. Don’t go out of your way to buy some limes, though.

I had one riff on the classic that was worth the extra ingredients, though. When we stopped into the Rosedon Hotel, they made us one that included burnt honey, cardamom, and orange zest. It was so delicious, and I could have had multiple.

A true Dark ‘n’ Stormy must be made with Gosling Rum. It’s officially trademarked by them, and it’s hard to argue with the original recipe. If you are going to make one, it’s worth getting the correct ingredients, especially when there are so few.

To make the rum floater stay on top, pour the rum in slowly. As with most layered drinks, the balancing act is delicate. A lot of ice helps, too. If you pour slowly, the rum will stay toward the top. I like to give it a gentle stir before I start drinking, without fully combining the two. I like getting drinks of pure ginger beer every once in a while.

Find your perfect rum-to-ginger-beer ratio. Dark ‘n’ Stormies are often 2-to-1 ginger beer to rum. If you like yours stronger, add a bit more rum. It all depends on how “stormy” you want your drink. When you make drinks at home, you are the bartender and can find your perfect ratio.

Now, if only I could sip on one of these back in Bermuda. That would be ideal. I wouldn’t even mind if it was during a storm.

I Rarely Recommend Gas Pressure Washers to Homeowners. This Westinghouse Is the Exception.

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

It gives you the performance of gas and the convenience of electric at an accessible price.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. Why Trust Us?

I’ve often said that gas pressure washers are the best choice for professional job sites, while electric pressure washers are a better fit for DIY projects and home maintenance. The Westinghouse WPX3400 pressure washer has prompted me to reconsider that advice. Here’s why: It delivers true gas power at an affordable price while minimizing the drawbacks of a gas engine.

Gas pressure washers traditionally deliver the most powerful cleaning, but come with a higher purchase price and recurring maintenance responsibilities. I’ve spent lots of time on job sites while workers huddled around a pressure washer, yanking on the pull cord and trying to troubleshoot a malfunctioning engine—not to mention the noise, weight, and fumes that come with a gas engine. But the WPX3400 is quieter, lighter, and more reliable than any of the other gas pressure washers I’ve used. It’s robust enough that it’s made me rethink my default recommendations for most homeowners.

If you’re a DIYer who wants maximum power and performance, and you aren’t deterred by working with a gas engine, this pressure washer will more than deliver.

The Westinghouse WPX3400 delivers powerful performance while minimizing the downsides of a gas-powered tool. Rated at 3,400 psi and 2.6 gallons per minute (GPM), it’s the most powerful pressure washer we’ve tested, both in terms of water pressure and volume.

I used the Westinghouse WPX3400 to clean concrete sidewalks, vinyl siding, and brick walls, as well as to refresh an earthenware chiminea and wash my truck (with a third-party foam cannon). The Westinghouse excelled on each of these surfaces. It had the power to clean stained and dirt-caked sidewalks, while its even spray and high GPM made car washing straightforward and hassle-free. Cleaning the chiminea and brick wall was effortless because the Westinghouse offers consistent psi. If pressure fluctuates up and down, it’s possible to damage surfaces such as brick or mortar.

So what about the downsides? This pressure washer has a gas engine, so it produces exhaust, and I certainly wouldn’t call it quiet, registering 89 decibels when idle. Still, that’s not overly loud in terms of gas-powered pressure washers, many of which can register well over 90 decibels. I expected the hard plastic wheels on the WPX3400 to make it louder and rattle-prone, but this issue seems to be counterbalanced by the soft rubber front foot. That same rubber foot seemed to help it withstand my durability tests, as well. The wheels did struggle some when moving across uneven surfaces and muddy terrain.

The Westinghouse pressure washer also went through evaluation for several months by Test Editor Paul Smith. “I spent the better part of the summer putting the Westinghouse WPX3400 to work on vinyl siding, a driveway, patio, and fence,” he said. “This thing is a workhorse. It has serious cleaning power, knocking all of that stubborn algae off the siding portion of my house that lives in the shade, my fencing, and all of my pressure-treated landscape timbers. There’s a lot of satisfaction in watching all of that green disappear with each pass of the wand and seeing the original surface restored.”

Smith found the WPX3400 to be relatively quiet compared to other gas-powered models he has used—benefiting both him and his neighbors. “If I have one gripe, it’s that I found the hose between the machine and the wand to be a bit short, which required a few extra moves of the machine,” he shared. “Beyond that, it’s a pressure washer perfectly suited to homeowners who want to wash a bit more—and faster—than they might with an electric model.”

One of the reasons that gas-powered pressure washers are popular on professional job sites is that they can take a lot of abuse. The WPX3400 stood up to everything I threw at it and kept running without a hitch.

I tried to find failure points by attempting to kink the hose and running it on an uneven surface, but the Westinghouse sailed through those tests. Next, I dropped it onto a concrete pad from a foot up. The rubber foot that also served as a drop buffer absorbed and distributed the fall impact.

I intentionally ran it out of gas, and I pushed it by using it with low oil. In all instances, it came back to life once I refilled the fluids. At the end of the testing, I winterized it—a straightforward if somewhat messy process of loosening a single bolt to drain gasoline from the float bowl (rather than the entire tank) and stocking the pump with antifreeze. It’s more winterization work than an electric model requires, but not much more. When I break it out next year for the buying guide refresh, I’ll get to find out how well it comes back to life.

If you need true power to deal with intense dirt and debris or if you have large jobs that would take hours with a less powerful washer, then this is a smart choice. And the user-friendliness of the WPX3400 minimizes the additional work of a gas engine. I have found small engines can be finicky to get started, and many require tinkering to keep them running. Not with the WPX3400, which has started easily every single time I used it.

If you dislike dealing with standard engine maintenance, or you need to store your pressure washer in your home over the winter, this machine may not be the way to go. And (just like any gas-powered option), it’s definitely not for anyone who plans to use their pressure washer indoors or in an area with poor ventilation.

Throughout my testing, the WPX3400’s power, durability, and ease of use impressed me at every turn. It’s my top recommendation for DIYers who are looking to make a pressure washer purchase.

Shop the Westinghouse WPX3400 Pressure Washer

At 3,000 psi and 2.0 GPM, the Greenworks Pro GPW3002A tackles tough jobs, making it comparable to many gas-powered models, minus fumes and maintenance costs. Its low center of gravity makes it stable and easier to move around on uneven terrain. Even after substantial rain, though, its 10-inch wheels never got bogged down in the mud.

Battery-powered pressure washers offer freedom from a power cord, and the HPW3204-2 even comes with a siphon hose to allow feeding water from a bucket, a well, or other water source. The Power+ HPW3204-2 offers three power settings, typically providing about 60 minutes, 45 minutes, and 20 minutes, in ECO, High, and Turbo modes, respectively.

If your cleaning tasks will be entirely in or around your property, there’s no need to worry about how mobile a pressure washer might be. I found the 100-foot hose could reach about 95 percent of my home and yard—simply by walking with the wand, with no need to wheel a cumbersome mounted washer.

Shiba Inu Absorbs 499B SHIB Dump From Binance Hacker

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Home » Altcoins » Shiba Inu Absorbs 499B SHIB Dump From Binance Hacker

The mainstream meme currency incurred a hefty sell-off due to the multi-billion dollar Binance hack.

The attacker behind Binance’s $6.4 billion security breach sold nearly 500 billion SHIB for roughly $1.96 million in ETH as part of a broader liquidation of more than 130 altcoins that generated an estimated $279 million in proceeds.

Despite the sale, Shiba Inu recovered from a low near $0.00000446 and has since been trading in a tightening range that technical analysts now flag as a potential breakdown setup.

On-chain trackers including Lookonchain reported that the address tied to the October 29 incident offloaded approximately 499 billion SHIB. The tokens were converted into 873 ETH.

The sale was one piece of a wider dump across altcoins, with the total proceeds from the attacker’s activity estimated at $279 million. Binance has said the breach stemmed from a compromised DevOps engineer’s laptop that allowed unauthorized withdrawals, and the exchange has pledged to cover user losses from its SAFU fund and company reserves.

The ability to absorb a sale of nearly half a trillion tokens without a deeper collapse stood out to some observers, even as broader market conditions remained soft following the Binance incident.

On the 4-hour chart Shiba Inu (SHIB) has been trading inside a rectangle pattern. Price recently broke below the $0.00000475 support zone after consolidating in a relatively wide range, a move that technical commentary interprets as rising bearish pressure.

A sustained breakdown from here would open a downside target near $0.00000300. On the other side, a recovery and close back above $0.00000500 would return the token to the prior range.

The combination of a large, forced sale from a high-profile security incident and a technical break below short-term support leaves SHIB sensitive to further selling.

JetBlue Hikes Checked Bag Fees Again To $50, A 43% Increase In Just 2 Years

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

JetBlue has raised checked baggage fees for the second time this year, increasing the minimum charge for a first checked bag on most North American and Caribbean routes from $39 to $50 each way. The new prices apply to tickets purchased on or after November 3, 2026. Compared with the $35 minimum in effect in May 2024, the latest adjustment represents an increase of approximately 43%. Passengers who wait until the airport, travel during peak periods, or check additional luggage will pay considerably more.

The increase affects more than basic economy customers. Travelers holding standard Main and Main Flex fares are also subject to the higher charges unless they qualify for complimentary baggage through an eligible fare, loyalty programs, or JetBlue credit card. The airline says the changes will help offset rising fuel and operating expenses while allowing it to keep advertised base fares competitive. The move could also prompt another round of baggage-fee increases across the US airline industry.

For flights within the United States, Canada, Latin America, and the Caribbean, JetBlue’s first checked bag now costs $50 each way during off-peak periods when purchased online or through the app more than 24 hours before departure. The price rises to $55 during peak travel periods. Customers who add a bag within 24 hours of departure pay $60 off-peak or $70 during peak dates.

Additional luggage has become more expensive, too, as reported by Thrifty Traveler. A second checked bag costs $70 off-peak and $75 during peak periods when prepaid, increasing to $75 and $85, respectively, when purchased closer to departure. A third checked bag carries a $200 charge regardless of the travel date. On transatlantic flights to Europe, the first bag on the cheapest economy fares starts at $80, with late purchases reaching $100.

Booking timing can significantly affect the final bill. A passenger checking one bag for a peak-season round trip could pay $110 by prepaying, or $140 if the bag is purchased at the airport. Tickets purchased before November 3 remain subject to the previous fee schedule, so the increase does not retroactively affect existing bookings.

The latest adjustment continues a series of increases that have made checked luggage substantially more expensive on JetBlue. In May 2024, the minimum fee for a first checked bag on domestic, Canadian, Caribbean, and Latin American routes was $35. It rose to $39 in March 2026 before reaching $50 in November. The change from $35 to $50 represents a 42.9% increase over roughly two and a half years.

JetBlue also varies baggage prices according to demand, booking timing, and route type rather than applying one universal charge. Peak periods include much of the summer, from late May through early September, as well as major holiday travel dates. March is the only month in 2027 without a designated peak travel date, according to the published schedule. This pricing structure allows the airline to collect more when demand is strongest.

Several customer groups remain exempt. JetBlue Mint passengers receive checked baggage as part of their fare, while TrueBlue Mosaic members receive one or two complimentary bags depending on their status level. Eligible JetBlue Plus, Business, and Premier credit cardholders can also receive a free first checked bag for themselves and up to three companions when the card is used to book the flight.

Delta Air Lines joins United and JetBlue in hiking checked baggage fees, following a sharp rise in global jet fuel prices.

JetBlue directly attributed the latest increase to rising fuel and operating costs. A spokesperson said the airline is adjusting baggage charges to help keep base fares competitive while giving customers the option to pay only for services they use. Higher fuel prices have placed additional pressure on airline operating costs. US carriers generally do not hedge fuel purchases to the same extent as some international competitors, leaving them more immediately exposed when market prices climb. Baggage fees offer one way to recover some of those expenses without increasing the headline fare displayed during flight searches.

JetBlue’s financial position adds another consideration. The airline reported a $257 million third-quarter loss, renewing questions about its ability to return to sustained profitability. It denied speculation about a possible bankruptcy last week. Ancillary charges can generate additional revenue without making the initial ticket price appear less competitive, particularly among customers comparing fares across multiple airlines.

JetBlue has previously been an early mover on baggage pricing. After its March 2026 increase, other major US carriers followed with their own adjustments within weeks. That history does not guarantee another industry-wide response, but it gives competitors an opportunity to raise fees without being first. For passengers, the latest change is another reminder that the advertised airfare represents only part of the cost of flying with checked luggage.

JetBlue has raised checked bag fees for the second time in 2026, making it the first U.S. carrier to do so twice this year. Economy travelers now pay $50 for a first checked bag bought more than 24 hours before departure, up from $45, and $60 if bought within 24 hours. Forester Pass, Through the Notch — John Muir Trail Day 5

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

A truly scary thing happened in the middle of last night.  I lay in my sleeping bag and suddenly realized I couldn’t remember the name of the highest mountain in the Lower 48.  Yes, the one I had climbed myself just two days previously.  I mean, I couldn’t think of it AT ALL.

I thought, “Ummmm… Mount McKinley, that’s in Alaska.  Mount Mansfield, that’s in Vermont.  Mount…”   I came up with Mount Whittier and I thought, “Maybe that’s it, Whittier.  Is it?”  It didn’t really sound right, but I honestly couldn’t think of the name.  I groped for it, but it just would not come to me.

Finally, I fell back to sleep and then, as I woke up, it gradually came to me.  “Oh, Whitney, yeah, Whitney, yes, Mount Whitney!”  But what was going on?  Was I getting  Alzheimer’s?  As I said, it was a very scary thing.

I also spent a couple of hours of my allotted sleeping time thinking about my sisters.  Sam urged that I find forgiveness.  I thought maybe I could write to them and say, “Look, I have a deep well of sadness and disappointment at how this has played out.  Yes, and anger too.”

“But there is also a well of forgiveness in me waiting to be awakened.  At some point in the future, in the near or far future, you may consider the possibility that you were not completely fair to your brother.  A little doubt may creep in about whether you honored your sibling relationship in a truly loving and caring way.  If that day ever comes, remember that well of forgiveness remains in my heart.”

Alas, the result of all that tossing and turning was that eight hours of sleep was actually four hours of real sleep.

Then the alarm went off, right at 4:30.  We wanted to get up early to go over Forester Pass.  At 13,153 feet elevation, it is the highest point on the Pacific Crest Trail and the highest pass on the John Muir Trail.  We wanted to get across before the weather changed.

The tendency had been for good weather in the morning and then, by late morning or early afternoon, the clouds built up, and the weather got stormy.

When the alarm went off, I felt like I hadn’t slept much at all.  Also, I was still coughing.  So I was not moving very quickly.

One brilliant move I made was to set up for breakfast in my sleeping bag.  For the first time on trail, it got cold during the night.  When I went out to pee, I thought it might be nearly morning and I moved my bear cannister close to my tent flap.

Back within the tent, I wondered whether I ought to insert my silk liner inside the sleeping bag for the first time.  I decided if it was nearly 4:30 I would not bother.  But the Garmin showed it was barely 12:30 – half past midnight!  Facing four more hours of attempted sleep in the cold I got out the silk liner.

Oh, my goodness, the sleeping bag liner makes such a difference!  It’s just wonderful, the difference between chilly and toasty.  It weighs almost nothing too; definitely one of my favorite hiking luxuries.

After the alarm, I had my “breakfast in bed.”  Then I lay in my sleeping bag for a while letting my digestion settle before going out to poop.  Less than ten minutes later I had assembled my tools and was facing the cathole I had so cleverly dug the night before.  All well there, I returned to my campsite and began to break it down and pack.

I was slow.  I don’t know why, because my pack was getting smaller with less food in it.  It should have been easier and faster.  In any case, our planned 5:30 departure turned out to be closer to 6:30, but still earlier than most of the campers in our vicinity.

We were on our way to Forester Pass.  I worried that it might actually be harder to tackle than Mount Whitney because we would cross with our full packs, not the lighter version we had carried to the summit.  As we left camp, the first thing we discovered was a sign indicating the pass was not four miles distant but five.

But it was a glorious morning, so beautiful.  Soon after our departure, the sun rose and the skies were blue; the glow shone across the mountains, across the boulder-strewn hillsides.

We cast long shadows in the early morning!

We even discovered a little early morning company!

How does Mr. Rabbit know that I am not a predator?

We ran into a guy named Marty, a carpenter from Salt Lake City.  He had decided that being a carpenter alone was not going to provide him with income in his old age.  So, he began buying dilapidated houses and fixing them up.  He then turned them into rentals.

Sam sensed the possibility of a political dispute.  He interjected diplomatically that it was time we kept moving.  Still, we walked with Marty for quite a long time as we gradually made our way up the valley, higher and higher, closer and closer to Forester Pass.

Along the way we met another hiker, Lauralyn.  She and her husband Doug own the Mt Williamson Motel and Basecamp, a lodging in Independence, CA famous among hikers in the High Sierras.  I wondered whether Sam and I might stay there a night when we got off trail.  She encouraged us.

Lauralyn seemed really nice. She was also inspirational.  She was no longer young and skinny, but her enthusiasm for hiking the High Sierra was unabated.  Her pace may have slowed, but her vigor and sunny disposition still glowed.  I really hoped we would stay at her motel, and I volunteered to recite The Raven for the owners and guests if we stayed there.

Meeting her brought home that Sam and I were just a couple of days away from going off trail at Onion Valley.  I asked her where the best steak was in the vicinity and she told us to go to The Seasons restaurant in Lone Pine.  I was salivating already.

Salivating, yes, but coughing too.  I was still coughing so much.  Blowing my nose frequently or hawking up big globs of mucus from my lungs.  It was disgusting and demoralizing.  Since starting out, I had better days and worse days, but overall, I had not felt healthy and well at all.  This respiratory infection just felt endless.

As we grew nearer, Marty pointed out a low-lying ridge that he thought was Forester Pass.  I disagreed, suggesting it might be a notch on a different ridgeline. As we got closer it became clear that it was the tiny, narrow notch.  The approach is a slow and steady uphill until the base of the mountain, when it becomes an almost vertical face.

The “notch” seen from below. If you look closely, you can see the outline of the trail passing just below the giant boulder filling the notch.

There was a stream at the base and Marty went on alone as Sam and I stopped to replenish our water.  The ascent is basically into a rock wall.  In 1930 they cut zigzag switchbacks going up the side of the cliff face.  It was a big engineering feat. At one point, a young man died in the process of making it.

Donald Downs was only 18 years old. He was a few weeks shy of his 19th birthday when he died, and his fellow workers created a plaque and left it there in his honor.

Photo courtesy of Lawrence Beck (since I forgot to capture it when I passed by)

I was amazed to have the energy to hike, especially as we began to steadily climb.  It was a little past 10:00 a.m. and our theory about “good weather in the morning” was about to take a hit.

It is astonishing, in the mountains, how fast the clouds can come in.  Suddenly, as we hiked upwards, we got pelted with graupel.  Those little pellets – neither snow nor ice — are not hard enough to be hail and not wind-driven enough to be sleet, not flaky enough to be snow, nor wet enough to be rain.  In a way, if we had to face precipitation, this was perfect because they hit us softly and then bounced off of us.  Neither of us really needed rain gear because it wasn’t accumulating on us.  On the other hand, it was not easing up either.

Not the best shot of it, but you can see the graupel landing and sitting on the plant’s leaves.

Finally, we reached the notch we had seen from afar and climbed the last rocky ascent to Forester Pass.  The moment was triumphant but short-lived, since the weather was bad and the thunder was thundering.

Sam said, “Let’s keep going,” and we did.  On the other side of the pass was a long, long, long downhill.

It began with switchbacks at first, all well above the tree line.  At that altitude the rocks are plainly visible and, as I frequently did on the trip, I asked Sam to explain the geology to me.  He studied the subject in college and made a career in it.  For my part, I am curious about everything and glad when I find a passionate expert who seems to welcome my questions.

We would look at a slab of granite, for example, and Sam would explain that granite is made of mica, quartz, and feldspar in varying proportions.  He got especially excited when we encountered crystal inclusions in the rock, which he termed phenocrysts.  In fact, I suggested that his trail name should be “Phenocryst,” though I am not sure it stuck.

When the conversation turned back to my sisters, I saw Sam get visibly annoyed for the first and only time.  He was talking about the Dalai Lama’s book, The Wisdom of Forgiveness.  In it, the Dalai Lama apparently discusses forgiving the Chinese for their atrocious behavior in overrunning Tibet.

I could not resist commenting that if there was any analogy with my sisters, then, seeing how little success the Tibetans have had in changing Chinese hearts and minds, maybe the lesson is that I should just give up on my sisters.  Sam clearly did not appreciate my flip remark.  He has such an even temper that I was astonished by his reaction and immediately apologized.

As we conversed and descended the graupel turned to snow for a little while and then to rain.  We passed some patches of snow left from the winter.

This was not the first snow we saw, but given our elevation I was surprised not to see more than we did.

We concluded the rain was not a passing shower, and I finally put on my rain poncho, and Sam donned his rain gear.

It wasn’t truly miserable, but it was not very pleasant hiking in the rain.  The next day we were going off trail at Onion Valley, where Sam’s rental car was parked, so we wanted to reach Vidette Meadow to ensure a moderate distance the following day.

As we hiked, we watched the weather changing in the mountains.  It is so unpredictable, because you cannot see what is just over the nearest ridge.  At times it felt like we were about to get clear skies, then it felt like the heavens were going to burst open in a thunderous flood.  In fact, it did neither, it just rained and rained as we descended.

Vidette Meadow was a large area with many potential campsites.  We checked out about ten of them, seeking a couple that were not too wet and soggy from the rain.  At last Sam found two fairly close together.  One was a very dicey spot right under a tree, but Sam said he wanted it.  Who was I to say no?

I took the other site which was wetter, but larger and flatter, and we set up.  It was still dripping rain when we started and then it came down a little harder while we were making camp.  So it was not terrible, but not so nice.  I was really tired.

Approving my campsite, with a little hill behind me…

It was a long day. Basically, I moved everything I owned into the tent. When the weather is nice, I leave a lot of my gear outside. But I thought, with the rain, let me just bring it all in.

I cold-soaked a beef stew, my last dinner before resupply.  Then I got ready for bed.  Sam was really cold the night before, when I used my liner, so I loaned him my puffy.  I was mostly using it for my pillow anyway, and I figured I could work out some alternative method for that with some odds and ends.

As I lay in my sleeping bag, I hoped the weather would be clear and dry the next morning.  I really didn’t want to pack out in the rain. We would go over Kearsarge Pass and back down to Onion Valley to pick up Sam’s car.

Then what?  I committed to doing this blog, even if I didn’t succeed in completing the John Muir Trail.  And I had to admit, completing the trail was in doubt.  The cough and the mucus and the endless, endless, respiratory infection were getting more-and-more debilitating.

Mount Whitney and now Forester Pass are achievements, nothing to sneeze at. But I had hoped and dreamed of the John Muir trail for at least a year.  To abandon that now would really be disappointing.

We will just have to see what happens.  Tomorrow is another day.

Hi, I’m Papa George! In my 69 years I’ve done a lot of cool things – I’ve crossed the Atlantic in a 40-foot sailboat, bushwacked 100 miles across the north slope of the Alaska Range, hiked for 26 days in Western Nepal crossing five passes over 5,000 meters, and climbed Mt. Kilimanjaro, to name a few. It was my honor to serve my country as a diplomat around the world for 34 years. I have been active in retirement, and I hiked the Wonderland Trail in 2024 and the Long Trail in 2025. This year I'm trying... the John Muir Trail!

Ubisoft Among Those Bidding On Marvel’s Blade Studio Arkane

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Months after Microsoft announced that Arkane, maker of Dishonored and Deathloop, would be among the studios it is divesting as part of Xbox’s current “reset,” the tech giant still hasn’t been able to settle on a buyer. Ubisoft is among the companies currently interested in buying the studio and potentially helping it complete work on Marvel’s Blade.

According to a new report by Bloomberg, the Assassin’s Creed publisher is among those currently in talks for a potential deal, having already submitted a bid. Microsoft is also reportedly exploring a sale of the studio back to its management. If no deal can be reached, a total studio closure is on the table. The terms of the proposals are unknown.

A spokesperson for Microsoft told Bloomberg it continues to explore options ahead of a December deadline for a decision.

Ubisoft and Arkane are both based in France, and it wouldn’t be the first time the publisher has worked on a Disney-owned license. It released Star Wars Outlaws in 2024, a game that seemingly failed to meet sales expectations despite being generally quite good.

The French publisher is facing its own headwinds, however. Ubisoft has seen ongoing layoffs and restructurings as it deals with revenue shortfalls and debt payments following big production delays. Despite employing over 15,000 people, it has no major blockbuster releases coming out this fall.

Last month, Microsoft revealed it had extended Arkane’s deadline for finding a potential buyer.

How to Style White Jeans for Fall

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

These outfits prove white denim has no off-season

White jeans are now a year-round staple, no longer relegated to the summer months between Memorial Day and Labor Day. From work-ready looks anchored by tailored denim to relaxed ensembles made for weekend getaways, these white-denim outfits do it all. This fall, take inspiration from a few of our favorite street-style looks. The lineup includes everything from straight-leg and relaxed fits to low-rise baggy silhouettes, in a range of soft hues like off-white and ecru. When paired with capsule wardrobe classics, such as a white T-shirt, a brightly colored button-down shirt, or a barn jacket, the results are stylish and equally easy to pull off.

Below, discover ten white jeans outfits that prove how cool they can look throughout the fall/winter months.

There’s a reason winter whites are a perennial favorite. Matching white jeans with your outerwear creates a clean, fresh monochromatic look. For a subtle sliver of color, layer an icy blue cardigan underneath, then add classic black pumps, and finish with sculptural gold earrings.

A simple way to wear white jeans during the fall is pairing them with a ladylike jacket in brown check print. The cropped silhouette offers a flattering counterpoint to relaxed denim, while black accessories—a roomy shoulder bag and ballet flats—lend a refined touch. Consider this an easy fall workwear formula you can wear on repeat.

If you’re a minimalist, try playing with contrasting textures to make your white jeans feel even more seasonally appropriate. Style them with a roomy black suede funnel-neck jacket, pointed-toe boots, and an unexpected accessory, like this elegant tassel bag.

The best fall outfits start with great basics: White straight-leg jeans, a white T-shirt, and a lightweight trench, which are all part of a well-rounded capsule wardrobe. Give these classics a hint of surprise by tying a pop of red knit around your shoulders and slipping into mules for an unexpected detail. They’re ideal for peak fall days, when it’s not too hot or too cold, and you can get away with a little peep-toe.

A striped long-sleeve T-shirt lends a touch of nautical charm to white jeans. Go for an oversized option in red to play up the relaxed, off-duty vibe. Black shoes—whether boots, loafers, or sleek sneakers—anchor the look, while a leather bucket bag and aviator frames complete the outfit.

Barn jackets remain a major trend for fall 2026. Make a case for wearing pastels well into the cooler months with a pale pink version and white jeans, offset by a black sweater, loafers, and a brown suede bag. Whether for a casual day spent running errands in the city or a weekend away, this combination strikes the perfect balance.

White jeans don’t always have to be worn in casual settings only. Make yours feel more elegant with a tuxedo-style blazer, silk camisole, and smoking slippers. A structured top-handle bag adds a polished finishing touch, taking the look effortlessly from desk to dinner.

Dip your toes into the season’s brightest color trends by putting a bright button-down shirt with otherwise simple styling pieces, such as a white T-shirt and straight-leg jeans. A leather belt and high-vamp shoes provide pared-back elegance.

French girls know how to layer the je ne sais quoi way, and denim is often the base. White jeans get a Parisian twist when paired with not one but three fall jackets—after all, temperatures are always unpredictable. A chore coat, plaid blazer, and windbreaker jacket can all be part of your layering essentials.

Nothing is more classically cool than wearing black and white during fall/winter. Bring your baggy white jeans into the winter months with a black turtleneck cardigan and sleek textural accessories—an embossed belt, suede flats, and a rich leather bag. Use this look as a base, then add a leather jacket or a tailored wool coat for extra warmth when needed.
